
The diagram makes the argument of the lesson before the prose does. A doctrine, by itself, produces nothing an art-and-culture paper can examine. A doctrine **plus a language decision, plus an order, plus a sectarian division, plus an endowed institution, plus a patron** produces manuscripts, monastic architecture, regional literatures and a living community - and all six of those are examinable.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Jainism is usually stored as three facts: Mahavira, non-violence, and two sects with a clothing difference. Stored that way it explains nothing, and it answers no question that an art-and-culture paper asks.

The reframing is this: treat Jainism as a **cultural formation** and ask what it *made*. It made a body of literature in a chosen language. It made an order that admitted women. It made monastic establishments that received royal land. It made a sectarian division through a documented institutional event. It made contributions to at least three regional literary traditions. Those are the answerable things.

### 2. The person, held to the evidence

**Vardhamana Mahavira** was born in **540 BCE** in a village near **Vaisali**, identified with **Basarh** in the Vaisali district of north Bihar. His father was the head of a famous **kshatriya** clan and his mother a **Lichchhavi** princess; they were connected with the royal family of **Magadha**. That connection is not decoration - high connections made it easy for him to approach princes and nobles in the course of his mission, which is a patronage fact of exactly the kind this session keeps naming.

He lived as a householder, abandoned the world at **30**, and wandered for **12 years**, not staying more than a day in a village or five days in a town. He attained perfect knowledge, **kaivalya**, at **42**; through kaivalya he conquered misery and happiness, and because of that conquest he is known as **Mahavira**, the great hero, or **jina**, the conqueror - from which his followers are the **Jainas**. He propagated his religion for **30 years**, travelling to **Kosala, Magadha, Mithila and Champa**, and passed away at **72** in **468 BCE** at **Pavapuri**, near modern Rajgir.

### 3. The doctrine, given at the depth this topic needs

Jainism taught **five doctrines**: do not commit violence; do not speak a lie; do not steal; do not acquire property; and observe continence, *brahmacharya*. It is said that only the **fifth** was added by Mahavira, the other four being taken over from previous teachers - his predecessor **Parsva** being named. Parsva had asked his followers to cover the upper and lower portions of the body; Mahavira asked them to discard clothes completely, which implies a **more austere** discipline.

Three further positions matter culturally:

- Jainism **recognised the existence of the gods but placed them lower than the jina**. It did not therefore have to build a polemic against deity worship; it subordinated it.
- It **did not condemn the varna system** as Buddhism did. Its position was that a person is born in a higher or lower varna as a consequence of sins or virtues acquired in a previous birth - and that through a pure and meritorious life, **members of the lower castes can attain liberation**. The reference literature notes that Mahavira looked for human values even in a *chandala*. Read this carefully: it is a doctrine of **liberation open to all** combined with an **explanation of social position**, and an answer that reports only one half has misrepresented it.
- Liberation is the aim, **no ritual is necessary** to obtain it, and it comes through full knowledge and action. **Full knowledge, action and liberation** are the **three gems**, the *ratnas*, of Jainism.

### 4. The four cultural decisions, each with an output

**Decision one: the order admitted both men and women.** Mahavira organised an order of followers which admitted both. Their number is given as **14,000**, which the reference literature itself describes as not a large number. That figure is worth keeping precisely because it prevents the opposite error - assuming a mass movement where the evidence records a compact one.

**Decision two: the language.** The early Jainas **discarded Sanskrit**, mainly patronised by the brahmanas, and **adopted Prakrit**, the language of the common people, to preach their doctrines. Their religious literature was written in **Ardhamagadhi**, and the texts were finally compiled in the **sixth century AD in Gujarat at Valabhi**, a great centre of education.

That decision has four traceable consequences, and they are the best answer this session can give to a "contribution to literature" demand about Jainism. The adoption of Prakrit **helped the growth of that language and its literature**. Many regional languages developed out of Prakrit languages, particularly **Sauraseni**, out of which grew **Marathi**. The Jainas composed the **earliest important works in Apabhramsa and its first grammar**. Their literature contains **epics, Puranas, novels and drama**, a large part still surviving as unpublished manuscripts in the Jaina shrines of Gujarat and Rajasthan; in early medieval times they also wrote extensively in **Sanskrit**; and, not least, they contributed substantially to the growth of **Kannada**.

Note what has just happened. A doctrinal decision - to preach in the people's language rather than the priestly one - has produced a literary history in five languages. That is the exact mechanism Lesson 14 will find again in the devotional movements. It is the single most transferable idea in this session.

**Decision three: the sectarian division, and the institutional event that produced it.** The split is usually taught as a difference about clothing. Its recorded occasion is institutional. According to a late tradition, the spread of Jainism in Karnataka is attributed to **Chandragupta Maurya (322-298 BCE)**, who is said to have become a Jaina, given up his throne and spent his last years in Karnataka as a Jaina ascetic - though this tradition is **not corroborated by any other source**, and must be reported with that caution. A second cause given for the southward spread is a **great famine in Magadha**, said to have occurred 200 years after Mahavira's death and to have lasted **12 years**, during which many Jainas went south under the leadership of **Bhadrabahu** while the rest stayed in Magadha. Afterwards, the southern group claimed they had strictly observed the religious rules during the famine, and alleged that the ascetics who remained in Magadha had become lax. To sort out these differences and to compile the main teachings, a **council was convened at Pataliputra** - modern Patna - but the **southern Jainas boycotted the council and refused to accept its decisions**. From then the southerners were called **Digambaras** and the Magadhans **Svetambaras**.

So the two names carry a *clothing* doctrine - Svetambara, "those who put on white dress"; Digambara, "those who keep themselves naked" - but the division is recorded as crystallising around a **boycotted council**, which is an institutional event with a place. That is a far stronger thing to write than "they disagreed about clothes".

**Decision four: the institutional base.** Epigraphic evidence for Jainism in Karnataka is **not earlier than the third century AD**, and from the **sixth century** numerous Jaina monastic establishments called **basadis** sprang up there and **were granted land by the kings for their support**. Jainism spread to **Kalinga in Orissa in the fourth century BCE**, and in the **first century BCE** enjoyed the patronage of the Kalinga king **Kharavela**, who had defeated the princes of Andhra and Magadha. In the second and first centuries BCE it seems to have reached the **southern districts of Tamil Nadu**; in later centuries it penetrated **Malwa, Gujarat and Rajasthan**, where a substantial Jaina population remains, mainly engaged in trade and commerce.

### 5. The comparison that must be made, and the reason for the difference

Jainism and Buddhism arose in the same region, in the same period, against the same background. They are constantly confused, and the confusion costs marks. Their difference is not arbitrary; it follows from one prior commitment each.

| Dimension | Jainism | Buddhism |
|---|---|---|
| Position on the gods | Recognises their existence but places them **lower than the jina** | Does not recognise the existence of god and soul |
| Position on varna | **Did not condemn** the varna system; explains position by past action while holding liberation open to the lower castes | **Attacked** the varna system; people were taken into the order without consideration of caste |
| Austerity | Discarding clothes; a **more austere** discipline than Parsva's | The **middle path** - avoid the excess of both luxury and austerity |
| Language of preaching | **Prakrit**, literature in **Ardhamagadhi** | **Pali**, the language of the people |
| Spread in early times | Did not win as much state patronage and did not spread very fast | Spread rapidly; transformed into a world religion under Asoka |
| Long outcome | **Still retains its hold** in the areas where it spread | Practically **disappeared** from the Indian subcontinent |

The last row is the examinable one, because it is counter-intuitive. The tradition that spread more slowly survived at home; the tradition that became a world religion did not. Lesson 5 explains the second half of that sentence.

**Why the difference in the varna position?** Because the two traditions located the cause of social position differently. Jainism kept a karmic explanation of birth-position and opened liberation to everyone regardless of it; Buddhism removed the criterion from the order altogether. Both are inclusive at the point of liberation; only one is inclusive at the point of social explanation. That is a real doctrinal difference with real cultural consequences, and it is exactly the sort of distinction that the instruction "do not flatten doctrinal differences" is protecting.

### 6. The boundary with the architecture and sculpture topics

Jaina **cave architecture, temple architecture, iconography and sculptural programmes** are owned by the architecture and sculpture topics, and the rock-cut heritage topic owns the excavated Jaina sites. What this session owns is the **formation behind the form**: the ascetic discipline that makes a withdrawn, excavated dwelling appropriate; the monastic establishment that requires endowment; the manuscript tradition that requires a library. When Lesson 18 uses an excavated site as evidence of doctrine dictating building type, it draws the *doctrine* from here and the *monument* from there, and says so.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** Jainism reaches this paper as a cultural formation - language, literature, monasticism, patronage and community - and as one of the plural traditions whose vocabulary is repeatedly tested.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** Two verified Preliminary items sit here and are routed to Ancient History, which owns the doctrinal reconstruction. A **2018** item asked which religious affiliation the **Sthanakvasi** sect belongs to, offering Buddhism, Jainism, Vaishnavism and Shaivism as choices; a **2026** item asked which of four named categories is **not** included among the four main forms of existence of life recognised in Jainism. Both are objective items about sectarian and doctrinal vocabulary; this session's contribution is Lesson 6's discipline on what a sect claim requires, and the honest note that the detailed Jaina classification of forms of existence is owned by the ancient-religion topic rather than reconstructed here.

**Probable framing.** "Assess the contribution of Jainism to Indian languages and literature"; "Why did Jainism survive in India while Buddhism did not?"; or an objective pairing of a sect with a tradition.

**Traps.**

- Asserting that Chandragupta Maurya became a Jaina as settled fact. It is a **late tradition, not corroborated by any other source**.
- Reporting the Svetambara-Digambara division as purely a clothing dispute, when the record gives a **boycotted council at Pataliputra** as its occasion.
- Saying Jainism condemned the varna system. It did **not**; Buddhism did.
- Dating Jainism in Karnataka from the Maurya tradition when the **epigraphic evidence is not earlier than the third century AD**.
- Describing the order as a mass movement when the recorded following is **14,000**.

### Revision notes

1. **Mahavira**: born **540 BCE** near Vaisali (Basarh, north Bihar); kshatriya father, Lichchhavi mother, Magadhan royal connections; renounced at **30**; wandered **12 years**; **kaivalya at 42**; preached **30 years**; died at **72** in **468 BCE** at **Pavapuri** near Rajgir.
2. **Five doctrines**: no violence, no lying, no stealing, no property, continence (*brahmacharya*) - the fifth said to be Mahavira's addition to four taken from previous teachers, **Parsva** being named.
3. **Three gems** (*ratnas*): full **knowledge**, **action**, **liberation**. No ritual is necessary for liberation.
4. Jainism **recognised the gods but placed them below the jina**, and **did not condemn varna**; birth-position is explained by past action, and members of the lower castes **can attain liberation** through a pure and meritorious life.
5. The order **admitted both men and women**; followers are recorded as numbering **14,000**.
6. **Language decision**: discarded Sanskrit, adopted **Prakrit**; literature in **Ardhamagadhi**; texts finally compiled in the **sixth century AD at Valabhi, Gujarat**.
7. **Literary consequences**: growth of Prakrit; **Sauraseni**, from which Marathi grew; the **earliest important works in Apabhramsa and its first grammar**; epics, Puranas, novels and drama; later extensive Sanskrit writing; substantial contribution to **Kannada**.
8. **Sectarian split**: late tradition attributes the Karnataka spread to **Chandragupta Maurya (322-298 BCE)** - **uncorroborated**; a **12-year famine** in Magadha sent many Jainas south under **Bhadrabahu**; the **Pataliputra council** was **boycotted by the southern Jainas**; thereafter southerners = **Digambara**, Magadhans = **Svetambara**.
9. **Institutions and patronage**: epigraphic evidence in Karnataka **not earlier than the third century AD**; **basadis** proliferate from the **sixth century** with royal land grants; Kalinga from the **fourth century BCE**, with **Kharavela's** patronage in the **first century BCE**; southern Tamil Nadu in the second-first centuries BCE; later Malwa, Gujarat and Rajasthan.
10. Jainism **did not win as much state patronage** as Buddhism and spread more slowly, but **retains its hold** where it spread; Buddhism practically **disappeared** from the subcontinent.
11. Boundary: Jaina architecture, iconography and rock-cut sites belong to the architecture and sculpture topics; this session owns the formation behind the form.
12. **Sthanakvasi** is a Jaina sect - a recorded objective demand, routed to Ancient History for its full treatment.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The cultural significance of Jainism lies less in its doctrines than in the decisions it took about language, institution and patronage." Critically examine. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely correct for a culture paper, though it understates the link between the doctrines and the decisions.

**The language decision is the strongest evidence for it.** The early Jainas discarded Sanskrit, patronised mainly by the brahmanas, and adopted Prakrit; their literature was written in Ardhamagadhi and finally compiled in the sixth century AD at Valabhi in Gujarat. The consequences are traceable rather than asserted: growth of Prakrit and its literature, Sauraseni out of which Marathi grew, the earliest important works in Apabhramsa together with its first grammar, a corpus of epics, Puranas, novels and drama, and a substantial contribution to Kannada.

**The institutional decision is the second.** The order admitted both men and women; monastic establishments called basadis proliferated in Karnataka from the sixth century and were granted land by kings; Kalinga patronage under Kharavela in the first century BCE and later settlement in Malwa, Gujarat and Rajasthan gave the tradition a durable base.

**The qualification, which the proposition misses.** The decisions followed from the doctrines. A tradition whose aim is liberation without ritual has no reason to require a liturgical language, and an order that admits the lower castes to liberation has a reason to preach in speech rather than in Sanskrit.

**Verdict.** Culturally the decisions are what we can examine; doctrinally they are what the positions implied.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the language decision with at least four named literary consequences 5 + the institutional and patronage base with two named regions and one named patron 4 + the doctrine-to-decision link stated as the qualification 4 + a verdict that does not simply repeat the proposition 2.** An answer that recites the five vows and the three gems without reaching a single cultural output cannot exceed five.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Jainism, like Buddhism, condemned the caste system, and Chandragupta Maurya's conversion proves its early spread to Karnataka." Identify both errors and give the defensible statement.

**Model answer:** Both errors are substitutions of a neighbouring tradition's position, and of a tradition's story, for the evidence. **First error - doctrine.** Jainism did **not** condemn the varna system; Buddhism did. The Jaina position is that a person is born in a higher or lower varna in consequence of sins or virtues acquired in a previous birth, while holding that members of the lower castes can attain liberation through a pure and meritorious life. That is liberation-inclusive and social-explanation-retentive at the same time, and flattening it into Buddhism's position erases a genuine doctrinal difference. Buddhism's position was different in kind: people were taken into the order without consideration of caste, and women were admitted to the sangha. **Second error - evidence.** The Karnataka story is a **late tradition** that Chandragupta Maurya became a Jaina, gave up his throne and ended his life there as an ascetic, and it is expressly **not corroborated by any other source**; moreover the **epigraphic** evidence for Jainism in Karnataka is **not earlier than the third century AD**. A tradition cannot "prove" anything, and where hard evidence exists it points several centuries later. The defensible statement: "Jainism did not condemn the varna order, explaining birth-position by past action while holding liberation open to the lower castes; its spread to Karnataka is attributed by a late and uncorroborated tradition to Chandragupta Maurya, but epigraphic evidence there is not earlier than the third century AD, and monastic establishments called basadis proliferate from the sixth century with royal land grants."

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that two movements which arose together, in the same region, against the same background, must have held the same positions. Shared context is not shared doctrine. The two traditions differ on the gods, on varna, on austerity, on the language of preaching, on the scale of early patronage and on their eventual survival in India - six differences, each with a reason. Writing "Jainism and Buddhism both opposed Brahmanical ritual and the caste system" collapses all six into one sentence and loses every mark that the differences carry.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Buddhism is the tradition candidates *think* they know, and it is the one they most often describe in a way that makes its own history impossible. If Buddhism was simply an egalitarian, rational, compassionate reform of a rigid ritualism, then its total disappearance from the land of its birth is inexplicable, and a candidate who has written the first sentence has no way to write the second.

The way out is to treat Buddhism as a **machine with parts**: a teaching, an order, a language, a representational convention, a patron and an economy. Each part did specific cultural work. Each part is also where the recorded account locates a cause of decline. Once you can name the parts, the rise and the disappearance are a single story rather than two contradictory ones.

### 2. The person, held to the evidence

**Gautama Buddha**, also called Siddhartha, was a contemporary of Mahavira. He was born in **563 BCE** in a **Sakya kshatriya** family at **Kapilavastu**, in the foothills of Nepal. His father seems to have been the elected ruler of Kapilavastu and headed the republican clan of the Sakyas; his mother was a princess from the **Kosalan** dynasty. He was born in a republic, and the reference literature notes that he inherited some republican sentiments - a small point with a large consequence for how the sangha was organised.

He left home at **29**, wandered about seven years, and **attained knowledge at 35 at Bodh Gaya under a pipal tree**, from which time he was called the Buddha, the enlightened. He delivered his **first sermons at Sarnath** in Banaras. He preached for **40 years**, resting only in the rainy season, and died at **80** in **483 BCE** at **Kusinagar**, identified with the village of Kasia in the Deoria district of eastern Uttar Pradesh.

### 3. The teaching, at the depth this topic needs

The Buddha is characterised in the reference literature as a **practical reformer** who did not involve himself in the controversies about the soul (*atman*) and Brahma raging in his time, and addressed himself to worldly problems. His statement of the problem: the world is full of sorrows, people suffer on account of desires; if desires are conquered, **nirvana** is attained - freedom from the cycle of birth and death.

He recommended the **eightfold path** (*astangika marga*) for the elimination of human misery, comprising **right observation, right determination, right speech, right action, right livelihood, right exercise, right memory and right meditation**. The path is attributed to him in a text of about the **third century BCE** - which is a text-layer fact, not a person fact, and should be reported as such.

The crucial cultural claim attaches to the path: a person who follows it **would not depend on the machinations of the priests** and would still reach the destination. That single sentence is the mechanism. It is not a mood, it is a *removal of an intermediary*, and it is the earliest instance in this session of the pattern that later devotional movements repeat.

Alongside the path he taught the **middle path**: a person should avoid the excess of **both luxury and austerity**. Set that against Lesson 4 and the contrast is exact - Mahavira intensified austerity beyond his predecessor; the Buddha refused both extremes. Two traditions, the same region, the same century, opposite answers to the question of how severe a discipline liberation requires.

He also laid down a **code of conduct** on the same lines as the Jaina teachers: do not covet the property of others; do not commit violence; do not use intoxicants; do not speak a lie; do not indulge in corrupt practices.

Two further positions have large cultural consequences. Buddhism **does not recognise the existence of god and soul (atman)** - described in the reference literature as a kind of revolution in the history of Indian religions. And, because early Buddhism was not enmeshed in philosophical elaboration, it **appealed to the common people** and particularly won the support of the lower orders **as it attacked the varna system**.

### 4. The sangha: an institution, with its openness and its limits stated together

The **sangha** was the religious order the Buddha organised, and its **doors were kept open to everybody, irrespective of caste and sex**. The only condition required of monks was faithful observance of the rules; on enrolment they took the vow of **continence, poverty and faith**. The three main elements of Buddhism are therefore **Buddha, sangha and dhamma**.

Buddhism's social impact is summarised in the reference literature precisely: it made an important impact by **keeping its doors open to women and sudras**, since both were placed in the same category by the older order and were neither given the sacred thread nor allowed to read the Vedas; conversion freed them from that mark of inferiority.

Now the limits, which must be stated in the same breath, because an answer that omits them is writing the harmony narrative in a Buddhist key. The same account records that **debtors were not permitted to be members of the sangha**, which naturally helped moneylenders and the richer sections from whose claims debtors could not escape; and that **slaves could not join**, which helped slave-owners. It further observes that the objective of the teaching was the salvation of the **individual** - and that while those who could not adjust to the break-up of the older tribal society were given a way of escape, that escape was **confined to the monks**, with lay followers taught to come to terms with the existing situation.

Finally, the account records a striking resemblance between Buddhist monks and the brahmanas they criticised: neither participated directly in production, both lived on alms or gifts, both emphasised carrying out family obligations, protecting private property and respecting political authority, and both supported a social order based on classes - with the difference that for the monks varna was based on **action and attributes**, and for the brahmanas on **birth**.

That paragraph is the single most useful thing in this lesson for a Mains answer, because it models the exact register this topic demands: **the inclusion is real, the limits are real, and both are named from the same source**.

### 5. Language, symbol and image: three representational decisions

**Language.** The use of **Pali, the language of the people**, contributed to the spread of Buddhism by facilitating the circulation of doctrines among common people. Same mechanism as Lesson 4, same mechanism as Lesson 14.

**Symbol.** Early Buddhist visual practice indicated sacred presence by **symbol rather than by body** - an empty throne, a tree, a wheel, a stupa, a footprint. This convention is owned in its art-historical detail by the sculpture-and-iconography topic; what this session owns is the **doctrine-to-convention link** and its later reversal. A verified **2026** Preliminary item asked directly what an **empty seat represents in early Buddhist iconography**, which is exactly this convention; the item is routed to the sculpture-and-iconography topic, and this session's contribution is the conversion argument used in Lesson 18.

**Image.** The reversal is dated and its cause is recorded: **from the first century AD** Buddhist monks practised **idol worship on a large scale** and received numerous offerings from devotees. In the post-Maurya contact setting a new form of Buddhism developed, the **Mahayana** or Great Wheel, **in which the image of the Buddha began to be worshipped**, and whose **doors were opened to all sections of the people**; those who did not subscribe to the newly founded sect came to be known as followers of the **Hinayana** or Small Wheel.

The recorded explanation of why Mahayana arose is a contact explanation: Buddhism in its original form was too puritanical and too abstract for foreigners, who wanted something **concrete and intelligible** and did not appreciate the philosophical doctrines emphasised by the existing schools. **Kanishka** became Mahayana's great patron; he **convened a council in Kashmir**, where the Buddhist teachings were **engraved on sheets of copper and deposited under a stupa** - and the reference literature adds, with exemplary honesty, that **the contents are unknown because this stupa has not been discovered**. Kanishka also set up many stupas in memory of the Buddha.

The art consequence follows immediately and is owned by the sculpture topic, but must be named here because it is the clearest case in the whole session of a **doctrinal change producing an artistic one**: Indian craftsmen came into contact with Greeks and Romans, especially at **Gandhara** in the north-west, giving rise to a kind of art in which **images of the Buddha were made in the Graeco-Roman style**, with the hair fashioned in that style. The influence spread to **Mathura**, which was primarily a centre of indigenous art, produced beautiful Buddha images and the famous **headless erect statue of Kanishka** with his name inscribed on its lower part, and also produced **stone images of Vardhamana Mahavira** - a fact worth keeping, because it shows a single workshop tradition serving two traditions at once. South of the Vindhyas, **Nagarjunakonda and Amaravati** in Andhra Pradesh became great centres of Buddhist art.

An important discipline attaches here. The reference literature says the later human image marks a change in **devotional practice**; it does not license the claim that aniconism was abandoned because sculptors became more skilful. Skill is not the variable; practice is.

### 6. The economy, and the five recorded causes of decline

By the **twelfth century AD** Buddhism was practically extinct in India, continuing in a changed form in Bengal and Bihar until then and vanishing almost completely afterwards. The recorded causes are five, and they are all institutional or economic rather than doctrinal defeats.

1. **The general pattern**: a religion inspired by the spirit of reform eventually succumbs to the rituals and ceremonies it originally denounced. Buddhism underwent that metamorphosis and became a victim of the evils it had fought.
2. **The reformed rival**: to meet the Buddhist challenge the brahmanas reformed their own religion - stressing the need to preserve cattle wealth and assuring **women and sudras of admission to heaven**. The rival closed the very gap through which Buddhism had recruited.
3. **The language reversal**: the monks were gradually **cut off from the mainstream of people's life**; they **gave up Pali** and took to **Sanskrit**, the language of intellectuals. This is the exact inverse of the decision that had spread the religion, and it is the most transferable cause in the list.
4. **The wealth**: from the first century AD large-scale idol worship brought numerous offerings; rich offerings supplemented by generous royal grants made the monks' life easy; some monasteries such as **Nalanda collected revenue from as many as 200 villages**; by the **seventh century AD** the monasteries had come to be dominated by ease-loving people and became centres of practices the Buddha had prohibited. A later form is named as **Vajrayana**.
5. **The vulnerability**: for their riches the monasteries were coveted by **Turkish invaders** and became special targets; many monks at **Nalanda** were killed, though some escaped to **Nepal and Tibet**.

One further recorded element must be handled with care rather than repeated as a slogan. The reference literature reports a saying attributed to the Buddha, addressed to Ananda, that the admission of women into the monasteries would shorten the religion's life, and it records that the presence of wealth and of women living in the monasteries led to further degeneration and that Buddhists came to look on women as objects of lust. This is a **reported tradition and a historiographical judgment**, not a neutral fact, and it must be attributed rather than asserted, and never used as an explanation in a candidate's own voice.

### 7. The abiding mark, stated as the reference literature states it

Despite its disappearance as an organised religion, Buddhism left an abiding mark:

- It made people **not take things for granted but argue and judge on merits**; to a certain extent the place of superstition was taken by logic.
- With its emphasis on **non-violence and the sanctity of animal life**, it boosted the cattle wealth of the country; the earliest Buddhist text **Suttanipata** declares cattle to be givers of food, beauty and happiness (*annada vannada sukhada*) and pleads for their protection. The reference literature adds an analytical judgment that the later brahmanical insistence on the sacredness of the cow and on non-violence was apparently derived from Buddhist teachings. Attribute that as a judgment.
- It produced an **economic diagnosis**: poverty breeds hatred, cruelty and violence, and the remedy was that farmers should be provided with grain and facilities, traders with wealth, and labourers with wages.
- It carried an **institutional and architectural legacy** - stupas, monasteries, cave complexes and the schools of Gandhara, Mathura, Amaravati and Nagarjunakonda - owned in their form by the architecture and sculpture topics and used here as evidence of the formation.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** Buddhism enters this paper as one of the plural traditions whose vocabulary, institutions, language choices and art consequences are repeatedly examined.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** Three verified Preliminary items sit adjacent. A **2020** item tested the relationship between the older Buddhist schools, the Mahasanghikas and the emergence of Mahayana; a **2023** item asked in which region **Dhanyakataka**, a prominent Buddhist centre under the Mahasanghikas, was located; and a **2026** item asked what an **empty seat** represents in early Buddhist iconography. The first two are routed to Ancient History and the third to the sculpture-and-iconography topic. This session supplies the surrounding cultural argument - why a contact setting produced an image-worshipping form, why the sectarian vocabulary matters, and how a representational convention converts into an art programme.

**Probable framing.** "Buddhism's decline in India was institutional rather than doctrinal - examine"; "Assess the contribution of Buddhism to Indian art and thought"; or a statement-pair on sects and schools.

**Traps.**

- Explaining the decline by "Hindu revival" alone, when the recorded causes include the **abandonment of Pali**, the **monastery economy** and **vulnerability to plunder**.
- Treating the shift from symbol to image as a gain in artistic skill rather than a change in **devotional practice**.
- Presenting the sangha as unconditionally open, when **debtors and slaves could not join**.
- Attributing to the Buddha, in your own voice, the reported saying about women shortening the religion's life.
- Confusing **Kanishka's council in Kashmir** - whose records were engraved on copper and deposited under a **stupa that has not been discovered** - with a surviving canonical compilation.

### Revision notes

1. **Buddha**: born **563 BCE**, Sakya kshatriya family, **Kapilavastu**; left home at **29**; knowledge at **35** at **Bodh Gaya**; first sermons at **Sarnath**; preached **40 years**; died at **80** in **483 BCE** at **Kusinagar** (Kasia, Deoria district).
2. Problem and solution: the world is full of sorrow, suffering arises from desire; conquer desire and **nirvana** - freedom from the cycle of birth and death - is attained.
3. **Eightfold path**: right observation, determination, speech, action, livelihood, exercise, memory, meditation - attributed to him in a text of about the **third century BCE**.
4. Its mechanism: a follower **does not depend on the machinations of the priests** - the removal of an intermediary, not a mood.
5. **Middle path**: avoid the excess of **both luxury and austerity** - the exact contrast with Mahavira's intensified austerity.
6. **Code of conduct**: do not covet others' property, commit violence, use intoxicants, speak a lie, or indulge in corrupt practices.
7. Buddhism **does not recognise god and soul (atman)**, and **attacked the varna system**; people entered the order without consideration of caste, and **women were admitted to the sangha**.
8. **Limits, stated together with the openness**: **debtors** and **slaves** could not join; the escape offered was **confined to the monks**; monks resembled brahmanas in living on alms and supporting a class order, differing in basing varna on **action and attributes** rather than **birth**.
9. **Three elements**: Buddha, sangha, dhamma. Vows on enrolment: **continence, poverty, faith**.
10. **Pali**, the language of the people, spread the doctrine; giving up Pali for **Sanskrit** later cut the monks off.
11. **Asoka**, two centuries after the Buddha, sent agents to **Central Asia, West Asia and Sri Lanka**, transforming it into a world religion.
12. **Mahayana** ("Great Wheel") arose in the contact setting, worshipped the **image** of the Buddha and opened its doors to all; those outside it were called followers of **Hinayana**. **Kanishka** was its great patron and convened a **council in Kashmir**, the teachings engraved on **copper sheets** under a stupa that **has not been discovered**.
13. **From the first century AD**, idol worship on a large scale; **Gandhara** produced Buddha images in the **Graeco-Roman style**; **Mathura**, primarily indigenous, produced Buddha images, the **headless erect statue of Kanishka** and **stone images of Mahavira**; **Nagarjunakonda and Amaravati** became great centres south of the Vindhyas.
14. **Five recorded causes of decline**: the reform-to-ritual pattern; the **reformed rival** assuring women and sudras of admission to heaven; the **abandonment of Pali**; the **monastery economy** - Nalanda drawing revenue from as many as **200 villages**, ease-loving domination by the **seventh century**, the later **Vajrayana** form; and **plunder** by Turkish invaders, with monks escaping to **Nepal and Tibet**. Practically extinct by the **twelfth century AD**.
15. **Abiding mark**: argument over acceptance; non-violence and cattle protection, with **Suttanipata** calling cattle *annada vannada sukhada*; an economic diagnosis of poverty; and a vast institutional and artistic legacy.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Buddhism's disappearance from India is best explained by the failure of its institutions rather than by the defeat of its ideas." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds for four of the five recorded causes, and needs one qualification.

**Institutional causes dominate the record.** First, a **language reversal**: Pali, the language of the people, had carried the doctrine, and the monks' later adoption of Sanskrit cut them off from the mainstream of people's life. Second, a **wealth problem**: from the first century AD large-scale image worship brought rich offerings, supplemented by royal grants, until monasteries such as Nalanda drew revenue from as many as 200 villages, and by the seventh century establishments were dominated by ease-loving people. Third, **vulnerability**: that same wealth made monasteries targets of Turkish invaders, and monks were killed at Nalanda while others escaped to Nepal and Tibet. Fourth, a **competitive institutional response**: the brahmanas reformed their own religion, stressing preservation of cattle wealth and assuring women and sudras of admission to heaven - closing the very access gap through which Buddhism had recruited.

**The qualification.** One cause is not institutional but structural: the religion's promised escape was confined to the monks, while lay followers were taught to come to terms with the existing situation. A tradition whose deepest offer is available only inside the order is exposed when the order decays.

**Verdict.** The ideas were not refuted; the vehicles carrying them - language, order, endowment and lay relationship - failed in sequence, and a rival reformed to occupy the ground. The tradition survived exactly where those vehicles held.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four institutional causes, each named with its own evidence 10 + the structural qualification about the monk-lay asymmetry 4 + the competitive-reform point stated as an institutional response rather than as "Hindu revival" 3 + a verdict that distinguishes refutation from vehicle failure 3.** An answer that offers only invasion and Hindu revival cannot exceed eight, because it has named one cause out of five and turned the second into a slogan.

### Concept check

**Question:** How should a candidate state Buddhism's social openness so that the statement is both accurate and defensible against a hostile examiner?

**Model answer:** By stating the openness and its recorded limits from the same evidence, in one movement. The **openness**: the sangha's doors were kept open to everybody irrespective of caste and sex; conversion freed women and sudras from a mark of inferiority, since both had been denied the sacred thread and access to the Vedas; the eightfold path let a follower reach the destination without depending on the machinations of priests; and the use of Pali carried the doctrine in the people's own speech. The **limits**, from the same record: debtors could not be members, which in practice helped moneylenders and richer sections; slaves could not join, which helped slave-owners; and the escape offered was confined to the monks, with lay followers taught to come to terms with the existing situation. The **resemblance**: monks and brahmanas alike lived on alms rather than production, upheld family obligations, private property and political authority, and supported a class order - differing in that the monks based varna on action and attributes rather than on birth. A defensible sentence therefore reads: "Buddhism opened its order to women and the lower orders and removed the priestly intermediary from the path, but the order excluded debtors and slaves, its liberation was in practice a monastic one, and its monks reproduced several of the social positions of the class they criticised."

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "open to all castes" as equivalent to "socially revolutionary". Openness is a statement about **entry to an institution**; social transformation is a statement about **outcomes in a society**. The two are different claims requiring different evidence, and the sources here support the first much more strongly than the second. Exactly the same distinction will decide whether your Bhakti answers in Lessons 9 to 13 score or collapse, which is why it is worth fixing now, in the easier case.

### Visual: five words that are not synonyms

```text
 THE TEST: can you name (a) what divides, (b) who is inside, and
 (c) one institution or text that carries it?  If not, you have a
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Objective papers use sect names as pure discriminators. A verified item offered the name **Sthanakvasi** and four traditions, and asked which one it belongs to. Another built statements around **Sthaviravadins**, the **Mahasanghika** and the emergence of **Mahayana**. A third asked where a named Buddhist centre under the **Mahasanghikas** was located. In every case the item is unanswerable unless the sectarian vocabulary is stored as structure rather than as loose names.

But the deeper problem is in Mains. Candidates write "the Bhakti sect", "the Sufi sect", "the Vedanta sect" and "the Lingayat movement" interchangeably, and each of those phrases is wrong in a different way. A **school** is not a **sect**; an **order** is not a **lineage**; and a **movement** is an analytical label rather than an organisation with a boundary. Getting these right costs nothing and buys precision in every paragraph that follows.

### 2. The five words, defined by what they divide

**School.** A school divides by a **position**. The six darshanas differ about what exists, how we know and what liberates, and Vedanta's sub-schools differ about whether Brahman has attributes and whether knowledge or devotion is the means of salvation. There is no membership roll; there is a position and there are people who hold it.

**Sect.** A sect divides by **discipline or authority within one tradition**, and it has an identifiable community with boundaries. The Jaina **Svetambara** and **Digambara** division is the clearest case in this session, because the record gives it both a doctrine of practice - white dress against nakedness - and an **institutional occasion**: the council convened at Pataliputra which the southern Jainas **boycotted**, refusing to accept its decisions. **Sthanakvasi** is a Jaina sect, and the verified 2018 objective item that names it tests exactly one thing - whether the candidate has stored sect names with their tradition. The full doctrinal treatment of that sect belongs to the ancient-religion topic, and this session records the affiliation and the method rather than reconstructing the doctrine.

**Order.** An order divides by a **rule of life and a chain of transmission**, and its members are initiates under a teacher. The **sangha** is one; the Sufi **silsilahs** are the other family treated in this session. Lesson 15 shows that they disagreed with each other on substantive matters - property, state proximity and initiation practice - which is why "the Sufis believed..." is almost always a false sentence.

**Sampradaya.** A lineage divides by a **founder plus a doctrine plus a transmission line**. This session carries four by name: the **Ramanandi Sampradaya**, described as the largest monastic group within Hinduism, worshipping Rama, with the sub-groups **Tyagi** and **Naga**; the **Brahma Sampradaya**, founded by **Madhvacharya**, associated with Vishnu as *Para-Brahma* and based on Dvaita Vedanta and Sadh Vaishnavism; the **Dashanami Sampradaya** established by **Adi Shankaracharya**; and **Pushtimarg**, founded by **Vallabhacharya** around **1500 AD**. The Kabir case shows the boundary between sect and lineage in action: Kabir is recorded as a **disciple of Ramananda** and part of the Ramanandi Sampradaya, and as later founding the separate **Kabirpanthi** sect.

**Movement.** A movement divides by **nothing formal**. "The Bhakti movement" is an analytical label for a family of regional developments with different chronologies; "the Sufi movement" is a label for the spread of *tasawwuf* through several distinct orders. Both are useful and both are dangerous, because a label with no boundary invites the claim that everything inside it shares a position. Lesson 9 shows exactly why that claim fails.

### 3. The named Buddhist divisions, stated at the level this session owns

The reference literature records that the **Mahayana** form arose in the post-Maurya contact setting, that **image worship of the Buddha** is its defining practice, that its **doors were opened to all sections of the people**, and that those who did not subscribe to it came to be known as followers of the **Hinayana**. It also records that the **Mahasanghikas** are associated with the development of Mahayana.

Beyond that, this session declares a limit. The finer relationships among the older schools - which school was an offshoot of which, and the precise doctrinal grounds of each division - are owned by the ancient-religion topic, and a verified 2020 objective item turned on exactly those relationships. This session does not reconstruct them, and a candidate meeting such an item should recognise it as belonging to the ancient-religion preparation rather than to the cultural framework taught here. Declaring that boundary is the honest move; filling it with a plausible-sounding chain of derivations would be the fabrication this session exists to prevent.

The **Ajivikas** enter this session from the other direction - not as a doctrine but as a **patronage and architecture fact**. The Barabar and Nagarjuni caves were excavated for them under Asoka and Dasharatha. That single fact does more work in Lesson 18 than a doctrinal summary would, because it shows a ruling house endowing a tradition that was neither its own nor the dominant one.

### 4. Discipline-divisions against doctrine-divisions

Here is the distinction that makes sect questions tractable.

| Division | What actually divides | What does **not** divide |
|---|---|---|
| Svetambara / Digambara | **Discipline**: clothing, and the authority of a council's decisions | The five doctrines and the three gems, which both hold |
| Ba-shara / Be-shara Sufis | **Attitude to Sharia**: in favour, or not - the latter called **Qalandars, Madaris, Malangs, Haidaris**, who hardly left any written accounts | The practice of *tasawwuf* itself |
| Chishti / Suhrawardi | **Discipline and worldly relation**: property, proximity to the state, and initiation practice | Membership of the wider Sufi tradition |
| Advaita / Vishishtadvaita | **Doctrine**: whether Brahman has attributes, and whether knowledge or devotion is the means | The authority of the Upanishads and the Prasthanatrayi |
| Lingayat / Veerashaiva | **Origin story, textual authority and social position** - see Lesson 12 | Shaiva devotional practice in the broad sense |
| Saguna / Nirguna | **Whether God has form and attributes**, and therefore whether image worship belongs | Devotion as the means |

Two lessons follow from that table. First, **a discipline-division is not a lesser division**: the Svetambara-Digambara split is permanent and communal, and it began over practice and authority rather than over metaphysics. Second, **a doctrine-division can coexist with shared authority**: Advaita and Vishishtadvaita both accept the Prasthanatrayi and both call themselves Vedanta, which is why the correct description is "sub-schools of Vedanta" rather than "rival religions".

### 5. What a sect claim must supply

Three items, always:

1. **What divides** - a named discipline, a named doctrinal position, or a named question of authority.
2. **Who is inside** - a community, an order, a lineage, or a regionally identifiable group.
3. **One institution or text that carries it** - a council, a khanqah, a matha, a sampradaya, a scripture, a canon.

Test any sentence you are about to write against those three. "The Bhakti sect opposed caste" fails all three. "The Lingayat tradition, founded by Basavanna in the twelfth century, is distinguished by exclusive worship of the Ishtalinga, has the Vachanas as its scripture, and is institutionally anchored to the Anubhava Mantapa at Basavakalyan" passes all three, and is therefore an answer.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** Sectarian vocabulary is the connective tissue of this whole syllabus area: it is how objective items discriminate and how Mains answers demonstrate precision.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** Four verified items are relevant and three are routed away. **2018** asked the tradition to which the **Sthanakvasi** sect belongs; **2020** built statements on the older Buddhist schools and the **Mahasanghika**-to-**Mahayana** development; **2023** asked the region of **Dhanyakataka**, a prominent Buddhist centre under the Mahasanghikas. All three are owned by Ancient History. A **2022** Mains Paper I demand asked for an analysis of the **salience of 'sect' in Indian society** against caste, region and religion; that is owned by Indian Society as a living-community question, and this session supplies only the historical vocabulary - what a sect divides, who is inside, and which institution carries it.

**Probable framing.** Objective pairing of a sect with a tradition; or a Mains demand where precision about school, sect, order, lineage and movement carries the distinction marks.

**Traps.**

- Writing "the Bhakti sect" or "the Sufi sect". Both are movements containing many orders and lineages.
- Reconstructing the derivation chain among the older Buddhist schools from memory. That is owned elsewhere, and guessing it is fabrication.
- Assuming that a division of discipline is weaker or later than a division of doctrine.
- Using "sampradaya" and "silsilah" as if they were the same word in two languages. One is a devotional lineage, the other a Sufi order, and they have different founders, institutions and practices.
- Treating "movement" as an organisation that could have a policy, a membership or a single position.

### Revision notes

1. **School** divides by a position; **sect** by discipline or authority within a tradition; **order** by a rule of life and chain of transmission; **sampradaya** by founder plus doctrine plus lineage; **movement** by nothing formal.
2. A sect claim must supply **what divides, who is inside, and one institution or text that carries it**.
3. **Svetambara / Digambara**: a discipline division - white dress against nakedness - crystallising around the **Pataliputra council** that the southern Jainas **boycotted**.
4. **Sthanakvasi** is a **Jaina** sect; its doctrinal reconstruction is owned by the ancient-religion topic.
5. **Mahayana** = the Great Wheel, defined by **image worship** and open doors, associated with the **Mahasanghikas**; those outside it were called followers of **Hinayana**. The finer derivation chain among older schools is a declared boundary, not reconstructed here.
6. **Ajivikas** enter this session as a **patronage and architecture fact**: the Barabar and Nagarjuni caves were excavated for them under **Asoka and Dasharatha**.
7. **Ba-shara** Sufis are in favour of Sharia; **Be-shara** are not, and are called **Qalandars, Madaris, Malangs, Haidaris**, who hardly left any written accounts.
8. Named sampradayas in this session: **Ramanandi** (largest monastic group within Hinduism, worshipping Rama; sub-groups **Tyagi** and **Naga**); **Brahma Sampradaya** (Madhvacharya); **Dashanami** (Shankaracharya); **Pushtimarg** (Vallabhacharya, c. 1500 AD).
9. **Kabir** illustrates the sect-lineage boundary: a disciple of **Ramananda** within the Ramanandi Sampradaya, who later founded the separate **Kabirpanthi** sect.
10. A **discipline-division** is not a lesser division; a **doctrine-division** can coexist with shared textual authority, which is why Advaita and Vishishtadvaita are sub-schools of one Vedanta.
11. The 2022 Mains demand on the salience of **sect** in Indian society is owned by Indian Society; this session supplies the historical vocabulary only.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Distinguish between a school, a sect, an order and a lineage in the Indian religious context, and show why the distinction matters for historical argument. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model

Each term names a different kind of division, and conflating them produces false claims.

A **school** divides by a position. Advaita and Vishishtadvaita differ on whether Brahman has attributes and on whether knowledge or devotion liberates, while both accept the same Upanishadic authority - sub-schools of Vedanta, not rival religions.

A **sect** divides by discipline or authority and has a bounded community. The Svetambara-Digambara division turned on clothing discipline and on a council's authority, the southern Jainas having boycotted the Pataliputra council.

An **order** divides by a rule of life and a chain of transmission: the Buddhist sangha, and the Sufi silsilahs, which disagreed among themselves on property and proximity to rulers.

A **lineage** divides by founder, doctrine and transmission: the Ramanandi Sampradaya; Pushtimarg, founded by Vallabhacharya around 1500 AD.

The distinction matters because it decides what a division can explain - a practice, a boundary or a position.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = four definitions, each tied to what it divides 4 + one named worked example per term 4 + the closing statement of why the distinction changes what a division explains 2.** An answer that defines the terms abstractly without a single named example is capped at four, because an unexemplified definition demonstrates vocabulary rather than command.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The Sufi sect and the Bhakti sect were the two main religious sects of medieval India." Explain why every noun in that sentence is wrong, and rewrite it.

**Model answer:** The sentence has three separate errors. **"Sect" is the wrong category, twice.** A sect divides by discipline or authority within a tradition and has a bounded community; neither label names such a body. Sufism in India is carried by distinct **orders** - Chishti, Suhrawardi, Qadiriyya, Naqshbandi, Shattari, Kubrawiya - which disagreed with each other on property, on proximity to the state and on initiation practice, and it is additionally divided into **Ba-shara** and **Be-shara** by attitude to Sharia. The devotional field is carried by **lineages** - Ramanandi, Brahma Sampradaya, Dashanami, Pushtimarg - and by regionally distinct formations with their own chronologies. **"The two main" imports a false symmetry.** These are not two comparable organisations; they are two analytical labels covering many bodies of different kinds, arising in different traditions with different institutional forms. **"Of medieval India" flattens the chronology**: the devotional cult is recorded as spreading from the seventh century AD, while the Sufi movement in India began around 1300 AD and reached South India by the fifteenth century. A defensible rewriting: "Medieval India saw two major families of devotional development - a set of regionally distinct devotional traditions carried by lineages such as the Ramanandi and Pushtimarg sampradayas, and the spread of Sufism from around 1300 AD through orders such as the Chishti and Suhrawardi silsilahs, which themselves differed on discipline, property and proximity to rulers."

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a convenient label implies a corresponding organisation. "Movement" is a historian's word for a family resemblance; it has no membership, no boundary and no position. The moment a candidate writes "the Bhakti movement believed" or "the Sufi movement held", the answer has attributed a single view to a category that contains contradictory views - and the examiner needs only one counter-example, such as the radical Naqshbandi order or the Tamil hymns' opposition to rival traditions, to show it.

## Lesson 7 - The six darshanas as a framework of inquiry

Progress: 7/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the orthodox six named and placed, the minimum content each contributes to a cultural argument, Samkhya's means of knowledge, Yoga's practice, Mimamsa's ritual soteriology and its social effect, the heterodox Charvaka position, and the firm boundary with the Philosophy papers

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the orthodox/heterodox classification with the six schools named, the Samkhya distinction of purusha and prakriti with its three means of knowledge, the Yogasutra's systematisation of citta-vritti control and eight-limbed practice, the Mimamsa account of salvation through Vedic ritual performed with understanding and its recorded social effect, the Charvaka or Lokayata materialist position with its four elements, and the recorded verification caution attached to four of the six schools
CA search: "Not required - no genuine current linkage for this lesson"
CA found: None; the single current record in this session belongs in Lesson 19
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: six schools, one frame, and the cultural work each does

```text
                      PURUSHARTHA FRAME
          Artha | Dharma | Kama | MOKSHA <-- the schools argue here
                              |
      ORTHODOX (accepts the Veda as supreme revealed scripture)
      ============================================================
      SAMKHYA        purusha / prakriti; three means of knowledge:
                     pratyaksha (perception), anumana (inference),
                     shabda (testimony)
                     -> cultural work: fixes what counts as evidence
      YOGA           Patanjali's Yogasutra (layered, debated date):
                     control of citta-vritti; eight-limbed practice
                     -> cultural work: a portable DISCIPLINE, not a
                        temple religion
      NYAYA          named in the six
      VAISHESHIKA    named in the six
                     -> this session carries them at the level of the
                        classification, and says so
      MIMAMSA        salvation through PERFORMANCE of Vedic ritual with
                     proper understanding; Sutras of Jaimini; Sabar
                     Swami; Kumarila Bhatta
                     -> cultural work: legitimises the ritual order --
                        and, as recorded, "legitimised social divisions
                        and reinforced Brahman dominance"
      VEDANTA        the end of the Vedas; Upanishadic inheritance
                     -> the whole of Lesson 8
      ============================================================
      HETERODOX (does not accept that authority)
      CHARVAKA /     "the materialistic view to achieve salvation";
      LOKAYATA       rejects the supernatural, God, Brahman and
                     salvation; recognises FOUR elements only --
                     fire, earth, water, air
                     -> cultural work: proves the tradition contained
                        its own denial, in writing
      JAINISM and BUDDHISM (Lessons 4-5)
```

Read the right-hand column. A culture paper does not ask what Samkhya proved; it asks what having such a school *did* to Indian intellectual and religious life. That is the column this lesson fills.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

The six schools are usually memorised as a list with one tag each - Samkhya/dualism, Yoga/Patanjali, Nyaya/logic, Vaisheshika/atoms, Mimamsa/ritual, Vedanta/Brahman - and then deployed in Mains answers as a decorative sentence: "Indian philosophy, with its six schools, has always emphasised spirituality." That sentence earns nothing, and it is also false in the interesting direction, since one of the traditions in the same intellectual world denied the supernatural altogether.

What a culture paper wants from the darshanas is three things: an accurate **classification** that you do not garble; enough **content** per school to explain a cultural consequence; and an honest **limit** where the local evidence thins out. This lesson delivers all three and refuses to pad the third.

### 2. The classification, restated and defended

By the start of the Christian era, Indian philosophy is split into an **orthodox school**, which treats the **Vedas as supreme revealed scripture** and comprises the six *Shada Darshanas* - **Samkhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa, Vedanta** - and a **heterodox** stream.

Two defences of this classification, both examinable.

**Defence one: it is a classification by textual authority.** It tells you what each school takes as an authoritative starting point. It does not tell you that they agreed about anything else, and it does not tell you that any of them was theistic. The reference literature makes the point sharply in the Samkhya case: the system **does not recognise the existence of God**, and holds that the world has not been created by God but by nature, with the world and human life regulated by natural forces. A school with that position sits inside the orthodox six, because the criterion is Vedic authority.

**Defence two: they share the frame, not the answers.** All six operate inside the Purushartha framework, and all six are, in the end, addressing **moksha**. They differ about what exists, how we know it, and what liberates. That is why Lesson 1 insisted they are schools of inquiry inside one tradition rather than six competing religions.

### 3. The content this session carries, school by school

**Samkhya.** It distinguishes **purusha** and **prakriti**, and it recognises **three means of knowledge**: **pratyaksha** (perception), **anumana** (inference) and **shabda** (testimony, hearing). The reference literature adds that the soul can attain liberation only through **real knowledge**, and that real knowledge is acquired through observation, inference and words.

Two cultural consequences follow, and they are worth more than the doctrine itself in this paper. First, a school that names its **means of knowledge** has created a shared vocabulary for argument, and every later dispute in this tradition is conducted in that vocabulary. Second, a school that reaches liberation through knowledge without requiring a creator god has set the terms of the argument that Bhakti later has to win: if knowledge liberates, devotion has to explain why it liberates better, or for more people.

The **attribution caution** applies in full: Samkhya is **traditionally associated with Kapila**, and the extant *Samkhya Sutra* is **late**, so authorship must not be reported as settled. A locally held general history gives Kapila a sixth-century-BCE birth date; this session records that as an unreconciled difference between its sources rather than adopting it.

**Yoga.** Patanjali's **Yogasutra** is a **layered text of debated date**, commonly placed in the early centuries BCE or CE. It **systematises the control of citta-vritti** - the modifications of the mind - and an **eight-limbed practice**.

Its cultural work is distinctive: Yoga is the one school in the six that is primarily a **discipline** rather than primarily a doctrine, and a discipline is portable. It requires no temple, no sacrifice, no priest and no endowment. This is why it later appears across traditions and regions without carrying its school with it - and it is exactly why the prohibition attached to it matters: **do not reduce every Indian yoga tradition to "union of individual and universal spirit"**. Several distinct traditions use the word, and the slogan flattens them into one.

**Nyaya and Vaisheshika.** This session carries these two at the level of the **classification**: they are named among the six orthodox, Veda-accepting schools. Unlike Samkhya and Yoga, these two - together with Mimamsa and Vedanta - are secured here only at the level of that classification rather than by a full passage-by-passage reading, so any specific doctrinal claim about them should be checked directly before it is used in a high-stakes answer. Lesson 8 supplies Vedanta and Mimamsa from other securely held passages. For Nyaya and Vaisheshika, this session states the classification and stops. That is a **declared limit**, and filling it with a remembered summary of categories and inference-types would be exactly the fabrication this session exists to prevent.

**Mimamsa.** Here the local evidence is substantive and it is unusually valuable, because it is the one school whose cultural effect is stated explicitly. Mimamsa holds that salvation comes through the **performance of Vedic ritual with proper understanding**; its texts and authorities named here are the **Sutras of Jaimini**, **Sabar Swami** and **Kumarila Bhatta**; and the recorded assessment is that the position **legitimised social divisions and reinforced Brahman dominance**.

That assessment is a judgment about a school's social function, and it should be attributed as such rather than asserted as a neutral fact. But it is enormously useful, because it gives a culture answer the thing it usually lacks: a school whose doctrine has a traceable social consequence. And it sets up the argument of Lessons 9 to 14 perfectly. If salvation requires correctly performed ritual, then salvation requires the people who can perform it correctly. Devotion, composed in the spoken tongue and requiring no intermediary, is the alternative to precisely that.

**Charvaka / Lokayata.** The heterodox materialist school is recorded as the propounder of **the materialistic view to achieve salvation**, rejecting the supernatural, God, Brahman and salvation, and recognising only **four elements - fire, earth, water and air**. A locally held general history adds that its exponent argued that **what is not experienced by man through his sensual organs does not really exist**, which implies that gods do not exist, and that India therefore developed **both the idealist and the materialist systems** of philosophy.

The cultural work of this school is enormous and is almost always missed. Its existence is the **evidence that the tradition contained its own denial, in writing, and preserved it**. An answer that says "Indian philosophy is essentially spiritual" is refuted by a school inside the same literature. An answer that says "the Indian tradition produced both idealist and materialist systems, and preserved the materialist one in its own compilations" has said something both true and demonstrable.

### 4. What the six did to culture, stated as four mechanisms

1. **They created a vocabulary for disagreement.** Once perception, inference and testimony are named as means of knowledge, a disagreement can be *located* rather than merely asserted. Every later debate in this session - including the Sufi orders' disputes with each other and the Vedanta sub-schools' dispute over attributes - is conducted in a tradition where disagreement is a recognised activity with rules.
2. **They separated discipline from doctrine.** Yoga's eight-limbed practice can travel where a doctrine cannot, which is why practice-level borrowing appears repeatedly in Lessons 15 and 17 while doctrinal merger does not.
3. **They tied salvation to specific social arrangements - and thereby made those arrangements contestable.** Mimamsa's ritual soteriology is the clearest instance, and its recorded effect of legitimising social divisions is the precondition for the devotional reaction.
4. **They kept a materialist position inside the record.** That is a fact about the *literature*, and it protects any answer from the timeless-spirituality opening.

### 5. The boundary, stated once and enforced

Doctrinal argument, objection and reply belong to the Philosophy papers. In this session, a school appears only where it explains **patronage, form, subject matter or practice**. Three consequences:

- Proof structures, category schemes and theories of error are not supplied here.
- Where a school's cultural consequence is recorded, it is taught in full - as with Mimamsa and with Vedanta in Lesson 8.
- Where the local evidence is thin, the limit is declared rather than filled - as with Nyaya and Vaisheshika above.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** The darshanas reach this paper as a **framework**: the thing that made a structured intellectual culture possible, and the thing whose vocabulary later movements argue in.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** The **2020** Mains Paper I demand on philosophy and tradition conceiving and shaping monuments and their art draws its "philosophy" half from this lesson and Lesson 8, and Lesson 18 executes it. A **2024** Preliminary item on the **Upanishads** - their content and their composition relative to the Puranas - is routed to the ancient-philosophy topic and anchored here by Lesson 3. The reference literature also reproduces an older Mains demand asking for a short note on **Charvaka**, which is answerable from the materialist block above.

**Probable framing.** "Discuss the contribution of Indian philosophical schools to Indian culture"; a comparative demand on knowledge against devotion as the means of salvation; or an objective item on a school's means of knowledge or its named text.

**Traps.**

- Equating "orthodox" with theistic. Samkhya does not recognise the existence of God and is one of the six.
- Attributing the extant *Samkhya Sutra* to Kapila, or dating the *Yogasutra*.
- Reducing every yoga tradition to "union of individual and universal spirit".
- Supplying detailed Nyaya or Vaisheshika doctrine in a high-stakes answer from an unverified memory - this session declares the limit instead.
- Writing "Indian philosophy is spiritual" when the same tradition preserved an explicit materialist school.
- Presenting the judgment that Mimamsa legitimised social divisions as a neutral fact rather than as an assessment.

### Revision notes

1. **Orthodox** = the Veda as supreme revealed scripture; the six are **Samkhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa, Vedanta**. Heterodox = does not accept that authority.
2. The criterion is **textual authority, not theism**: Samkhya does **not recognise the existence of God** and holds the world to be produced and regulated by nature.
3. **Samkhya**: distinguishes **purusha** and **prakriti**; three means of knowledge - **pratyaksha, anumana, shabda**; liberation through **real knowledge**.
4. Samkhya's attribution to **Kapila** is traditional; the extant *Samkhya Sutra* is **late**. A locally held general history gives Kapila a sixth-century-BCE birth; this session records the difference rather than adopting it.
5. **Yoga**: the **Yogasutra**, layered and of debated date, systematises **citta-vritti** control and an **eight-limbed practice**. It is a **portable discipline**, which is why practice-level borrowing travels while doctrine does not.
6. Do **not** reduce every Indian yoga tradition to "union of individual and universal spirit".
7. **Nyaya and Vaisheshika** are carried here **at classification level only** - a declared limit, because the evidence used here secures them no further.
8. **Mimamsa**: salvation through **performance of Vedic ritual with proper understanding**; **Sutras of Jaimini**, **Sabar Swami**, **Kumarila Bhatta**; recorded as having **legitimised social divisions and reinforced Brahman dominance** - an assessment, to be attributed.
9. **Charvaka / Lokayata**: the materialist view; rejects the supernatural, God, Brahman and salvation; recognises **four elements - fire, earth, water, air**; holds that what is not experienced through the senses does not exist.
10. The tradition therefore produced **both idealist and materialist systems** and preserved both - which refutes the timeless-spirituality opening.
11. Four cultural mechanisms: a vocabulary for disagreement; discipline separable from doctrine; salvation tied to social arrangements and therefore contestable; a materialist position kept inside the record.
12. Boundary: proofs, objections and replies belong to the Philosophy papers; this session uses a school only where it explains patronage, form, subject matter or practice.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The six darshanas mattered to Indian culture less for what they concluded than for what they made possible." Discuss. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is well founded, provided "made possible" is read institutionally and socially rather than vaguely.

**First, they made disagreement a structured activity.** Samkhya names three means of knowledge - perception, inference and testimony - and once a tradition has named its criteria, a dispute can be located rather than merely asserted. Later disputes, from the Vedanta sub-schools' quarrel over whether Brahman has attributes to the Sufi orders' disagreements over property and state proximity, occur in a culture where argument is a recognised practice.

**Second, they made discipline separable from doctrine.** Yoga's eight-limbed practice, systematised in a layered text of debated date, requires no temple, priest or endowment. A portable discipline travels where a school cannot, which is why practice-level borrowing recurs across traditions while doctrinal merger does not.

**Third, they made the social order contestable by tying salvation to it.** Mimamsa holds salvation to follow from Vedic ritual performed with proper understanding, a position assessed as legitimising social divisions and reinforcing Brahman dominance. Precisely because salvation was thus tied to performance, an alternative means - devotion in the spoken tongue, requiring no intermediary - became a socially consequential proposal.

**Fourth, they kept the tradition's own denial in the record.** The Charvaka or Lokayata school rejected the supernatural, God, Brahman and salvation, recognising only fire, earth, water and air.

**Qualification.** Conclusions were not irrelevant: whether Brahman has attributes decided whether image worship had a philosophical warrant.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the "made possible" thesis stated institutionally 3 + four mechanisms, each with a named school and its content 8 + the Charvaka counter-evidence used against the timeless-spirituality opening 2 + a qualification conceding that one conclusion had direct cultural consequences 2.** An answer that lists the six schools with one tag each and no mechanism cannot exceed four.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the classification "orthodox and heterodox" not tell you which schools were theistic, and what does it tell you instead?

**Model answer:** Because the classification is drawn on a single criterion - **acceptance of the Vedas as supreme revealed scripture** - and that criterion is about a source of authority, not about the existence of a god. The clearest demonstration is Samkhya: it sits among the six orthodox darshanas, yet it does **not recognise the existence of God**, holding that the world has not been created by God but by nature, and that the world and human life are regulated by natural forces; liberation comes through real knowledge, acquired through observation, inference and words. Conversely, the heterodox side is not uniform either: Jainism recognises the existence of the gods while placing them below the jina, Buddhism does not recognise god or soul, and Charvaka denies the supernatural altogether while recognising only four elements. What the classification *does* tell you is far more useful for a culture answer: it tells you **which body of texts a school must argue from**, and therefore who has authority to interpret, what language that interpretation occurs in, and which social group holds the interpretive role - all of which are the preconditions of the devotional reaction taught later.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading a classification as a ranking or as a description of belief. "Orthodox" here is not a compliment and "heterodox" is not a criticism; neither word reports what a school thought about God, about ritual or about caste. A candidate who reads the labels as belief-descriptions will write that the orthodox schools were theistic and ritualist and the heterodox ones rationalist - and will be contradicted by Samkhya on one side and by Jainism's gods on the other, in the same paragraph.

## Lesson 8 - Vedanta and its sub-schools: attributes, means, and what each produced on the ground

Progress: 8/20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the parent school and the Prasthanatrayi, Advaita with Shankaracharya's institutions, Vishishtadvaita with Ramanuja's devotional consequence, Dvaita with the Brahma Sampradaya lineage, the one-line contrast, and the conversion of a doctrinal difference into temple culture
