### Visual: two regions, two clocks, one shared lesson

```text
 ===================== KARNATAKA: THE 12th CENTURY ===================
 BASAVANNA, 12th century CE, founder of the LINGAYAT tradition
   doctrine  : exclusive worship of the ISHTALINGA, a symbol of the
               FORMLESS divine
   society   : egalitarian; rejection of caste and elaborate ritual;
               strong emphasis on EQUALITY, ANTI-CASTE positions and
               WOMEN'S PARTICIPATION; POST-PUBERTY MARRIAGE and
               RE-MARRIAGE encouraged
   scripture : the VACHANAS  (free-verse utterance, in KANNADA)
   institution: ANUBHAVA MANTAPA at BASAVAKALYAN, Bidar district --
               "the world's inaugural spiritual assembly", led by the
               sage ALLAMA PRABHU, recognised as THE BIRTHPLACE OF
               VACHANA LITERATURE
                                |
 !! NOT THE SAME AS !!          |
 VEERASHAIVA: the traditional community is said to PRE-DATE
   Basavanna; traces its origin to the PANCHACHARYAS -- RENUKA,
   DARUKA, GHANTAKARNA, DHENUKARNA, VISHVAKARNA; ACCEPTS SOME VEDIC
   TRADITION and MAY STILL ACKNOWLEDGE VARNA
 =====================================================================
 ===================== MAHARASHTRA: A CLOCK OF ITS OWN ===============
 PERIOD 1  c. 1200-1350   JNANESHWAR 1275-96 | NAMDEV 1270-1350
 DORMANT   mid-14th c. to the early 16th c.
 PERIOD 2  16th century   EKNATH -- "the seed of literary genius
                          germinated again"
 PERIOD 3  early 17th c. to the close of Peshwa rule -- "MOST
           BRILLIANT"      TUKARAM 1598-1650 | RAMDAS, 17th c.
 =====================================================================
   THE SHARED LESSON: a region can have its own chronology, its own
   language, its own scripture-form and its own institution. A
   single "Bhakti movement" timeline cannot hold any of this.
```

### 1. The learner's actual problem

These two regions are where the single-movement narrative visibly breaks, and where two of the most reliable traps in the topic live.

The first trap is **Lingayat/Veerashaiva**. Treated as synonyms, they produce a false statement about origin, textual authority and social position simultaneously - a three-error sentence.

The second trap is **the Maharashtra clock**. A candidate carrying a single timeline cannot make sense of a tradition that flourishes, goes dormant for roughly 150 years, revives, and then has its most brilliant phase three centuries after it began.

And behind both sits a doctrinal awkwardness worth meeting head-on: the **Ishtalinga is a symbol of the formless divine**, inside a school usually classed as Shaiva-devotional. The binary of Lesson 11 does not cleanly hold it, and the correct move is to say so rather than to force it.

### 2. Basavanna and the Lingayat tradition

**Basavanna**, of the **12th century CE**, is the founder of the **Lingayat** tradition. Its features, stated exactly:

- **Exclusive worship of the Ishtalinga**, a symbol of the **formless divine**.
- An **egalitarian society**; **rejection of caste and elaborate ritual**.
- **Strong emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and women's participation**, with **post-puberty marriage and re-marriage encouraged**.
- Scripture: the **Vachanas**.
- Institution: the **Anubhava Mantapa** at **Basavakalyan**, in **Bidar district**, described as **"the world's inaugural spiritual assembly"**, led by the sage **Allama Prabhu**, and recognised as **the birthplace of Vachana literature**.

Notice how much of that is *institutional and social* rather than doctrinal - an assembly with a named leader and a named place, a named scripture-form, and named marriage practices. That is why this tradition is so valuable for a culture answer: it converts "egalitarian ideals" into an assembly, a literature and a set of household practices.

The **Vachana** is a named form - free-verse utterance, in Kannada - and the reference literature records that Vachana literature **played a pivotal role in disseminating the principles of Veerashaivism**. Read that sentence carefully; it is the reason the next section exists.

### 3. The distinction that must never be collapsed

**Lingayats are not the same as Veerashaivas.** The traditional Veerashaiva community:

- is said to **pre-date Basavanna**;
- traces its origin to the **Panchacharyas** - **Renuka, Daruka, Ghantakarna, Dhenukarna, Vishvakarna**;
- **accepts some Vedic tradition**; and
- **may still acknowledge varna**.

So the two differ on **origin story**, on **textual authority** and on **social position** - three separate axes. And the prohibition follows immediately: **do not project Basavanna's anti-caste position onto the older Veerashaiva tradition**, which may still acknowledge varna.

This is also a superb small example of the Lesson 6 distinction at work. What divides the two is not a single doctrine; it is a cluster of origin, authority and social position - which is exactly what a **sect** division looks like, as against a **school** division that turns on one position.

### 4. The Ishtalinga and the limits of a binary

Here is the awkwardness, stated rather than hidden. The **Ishtalinga is a symbol of the formless divine** - and it is worshipped as an object, exclusively, within a tradition normally classed as Shaiva-devotional.

By Lesson 11's binary, formlessness points to Nirguna and object-worship points to Saguna. The tradition does both. The instruction is therefore explicit: **do not force it into the Saguna/Nirguna binary**.

Why does this matter beyond taxonomy? Because it demonstrates that **the binary is an analytical device applied to the material, not a description the material gives of itself**. Lesson 1 flagged this; here is the case that proves it. A candidate who can name a tradition the binary does not fit has understood the binary better than one who applies it everywhere.

### 5. Maharashtra: the Varkari stream, saint by saint

| Saint | Dates | Language | Contribution |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Jnaneshwar** (Gyaneshwar) | **1275-96** | Marathi | The **oldest known work in Marathi** is his, from the 13th century; **credited with beginning the kirtan** in Maharashtra; wrote a detailed **commentary on the Bhagavad Gita in Marathi**. A prominent figure of the sect whose pilgrimage carries the **Ringan** and **Dhava** events |
| **Namdev** | **1270-1350** | Marathi | Wrote "a great many **Abhangas**" on devotion to God; named among the **Bhagats** in the **Adi Granth** - a Marathi saint entering the Punjabi canon. **Abhanga** is recorded as a Maharashtra song form "in praise of the God Vithobha", composed and sung by Tukaram and Namdev |
| **Eknath** | **Sixteenth century** - two conflicting dates recorded | Marathi | With him "the seed of literary genius germinated again" after the gap from the mid-14th to the early 16th century |
| **Tukaram** | **1598-1650** | Marathi | "The greatest poet in Marathi literature"; preached **asceticism, toleration and devotion to God**; composed **Abhanga** poetry compiled as the **Tukaramgatha**; discussed the conflict between **Pravritti and Nivritti** - passion for life, family and business against the desire to renounce and seek Moksha |
| **Ramdas** | 17th century | Marathi | Wrote **Dasbodh**, "containing sermons on abstract as well as practical topics". Political readings of Ramdas belong to Medieval History, not here |

Three things in that table deserve separate emphasis.

**"Credited with" and "oldest known" are hedged terms.** "Credited with beginning the kirtan" is an attribution formula, and "the oldest known work in Marathi" is a **survival statement** - it reports what has come down to us, not what was written. Both hedges must be carried.

**Namdev crosses a canon boundary.** A Marathi saint whose compositions enter the Punjabi **Adi Granth** is, by itself, evidence of cross-regional literary circulation - and Lesson 17 uses exactly this kind of evidence, because it is a **text** fact rather than a claim about merged belief.

**Tukaram's Pravritti-Nivritti conflict is a doctrinal content, not a mood.** The tension between engagement with life, family and business and the desire to renounce and seek Moksha is a structured problem, and naming it converts "Tukaram wrote devotional poetry" into "Tukaram staged a specific philosophical conflict in Marathi verse".

A further recognition-clock fact attaches to Tukaram and is used in Lesson 20: the **Sant Tukaram Shila Mandir** at **Dehu**, Pune district, was **inaugurated in 2022**.

### 6. The periodisation, and why it is worth marks

Marathi devotional literature is divided into three phases: an **early period c. 1200-1350**; a **dormant phase from the mid-fourteenth to the early sixteenth century**; and a **"most brilliant" third period from the early seventeenth century to the close of Peshwa rule**.

Why is this worth stating in an answer? Because **a Bhakti answer that carries one chronology is weaker than one that shows the movement had different clocks in different regions.** The Maharashtra sequence - flourish, dormancy, revival, brilliance - is not compatible with a relay narrative in which devotion arrives once and stays. It is compatible with the causal model of Lesson 9, in which conditions recur.

### 7. The date conflict, handled in the house style

**Eknath's exact lifespan is prohibited.** The same reference work gives **"Eknath (1533-99)"** among the Varkari saints and **"Saint Eknath was born in 1518"** in its Marathi literature chapter. Both are the work's own and neither is corrected by it, so the conflict is **open and unresolved**.

The rule: write "**sixteenth-century Marathi saint of the Varkari tradition**" plus his contribution; report **both** dates with their locations **only** when the demand is explicitly chronological; **never** present either as *the* date; and **never** build a chronology, a contemporaneity claim or a relative-ordering argument on it.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** These two regions supply the strongest evidence that Indian devotion is a set of regional literatures and institutions rather than a single national movement.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** The **2021** Paper I demand on the **nature of Bhakti literature and its contribution to Indian culture** draws two of its strongest units from here - Jnaneshwar's Marathi commentary and kirtan, and the Abhanga tradition of Namdev and Tukaram - and the **Vachana** unit from Karnataka; the demand is routed to Medieval History as owner. The **2019** Preliminary saint-chronology item is defeated by the periodisation discipline taught here.

**Probable framing.** "Regional devotional traditions followed their own chronologies - examine"; "Assess the contribution of a named regional tradition to its language"; or an objective pairing of a saint, a form and a language.

**Traps.**

- Equating **Lingayats with Veerashaivas**, or projecting Basavanna's anti-caste position onto the older community.
- Forcing the **Ishtalinga** into the Saguna/Nirguna binary.
- Giving **Eknath** a definite lifespan.
- Dropping the hedges in "credited with beginning the kirtan" and "the oldest known work in Marathi".
- Carrying one chronology across all regions.
- Importing political readings of **Ramdas**, which belong to Medieval History.

### Revision notes

1. **Basavanna**, **12th century CE**, founded the **Lingayat** tradition: exclusive worship of the **Ishtalinga**, a symbol of the **formless divine**; egalitarian society; rejection of caste and elaborate ritual; emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and **women's participation**; **post-puberty marriage and re-marriage encouraged**.
2. Scripture: the **Vachanas**. Institution: the **Anubhava Mantapa** at **Basavakalyan, Bidar district** - "the world's inaugural spiritual assembly", led by **Allama Prabhu**, recognised as **the birthplace of Vachana literature**.
3. Vachana literature **played a pivotal role in disseminating the principles of Veerashaivism**.
4. **Lingayats are not Veerashaivas**: the traditional Veerashaiva community is said to **pre-date Basavanna**, traces its origin to the **Panchacharyas - Renuka, Daruka, Ghantakarna, Dhenukarna, Vishvakarna** - **accepts some Vedic tradition** and **may still acknowledge varna**.
5. Do **not** force the **Ishtalinga** into the Saguna/Nirguna binary; the binary is an analytical device, not the material's self-description.
6. **Jnaneshwar 1275-96**: the **oldest known work in Marathi**; **credited with beginning the kirtan**; a detailed **Bhagavad Gita commentary in Marathi**; the sect's pilgrimage carries **Ringan** and **Dhava**.
7. **Namdev 1270-1350**: many **Abhangas**; named among the **Bhagats** in the **Adi Granth**. **Abhanga** is a Maharashtra song form "in praise of the God Vithobha", composed and sung by **Tukaram and Namdev**.
8. **Eknath**: **sixteenth century**; with him "the seed of literary genius germinated again". **Two conflicting dates** are recorded - 1533-99 and born 1518 - and the conflict is **unresolved**.
9. **Tukaram 1598-1650**: "the greatest poet in Marathi literature"; preached **asceticism, toleration and devotion**; **Abhanga** poetry compiled as the **Tukaramgatha**; staged the **Pravritti versus Nivritti** conflict. The **Sant Tukaram Shila Mandir** at **Dehu** was inaugurated in **2022**.
10. **Ramdas**, 17th century: **Dasbodh**, sermons on abstract and practical topics. Political readings route to Medieval History.
11. **Marathi periodisation**: early **c. 1200-1350**; **dormant** mid-14th to early 16th century; **most brilliant** from the early 17th century to the close of Peshwa rule.
12. A Bhakti answer carrying **one** chronology is weaker than one showing **different clocks in different regions**.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Regional devotional traditions are better understood through their institutions and literatures than through a single all-India chronology." Examine with reference to Karnataka and Maharashtra. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition holds, and the two regions demonstrate it from opposite directions.

**Karnataka gives an institution.** Basavanna, of the twelfth century CE, founded the Lingayat tradition on exclusive worship of the Ishtalinga - a symbol of the formless divine - with rejection of caste and elaborate ritual, emphasis on equality and women's participation, and encouragement of post-puberty marriage and re-marriage. Its scripture is the Vachanas, and its institutional anchor is the Anubhava Mantapa at Basavakalyan in Bidar district, described as the world's inaugural spiritual assembly, led by Allama Prabhu and recognised as the birthplace of Vachana literature. Crucially, the older Veerashaiva community is said to pre-date Basavanna, traces its origin to the Panchacharyas, accepts some Vedic tradition and may still acknowledge varna - so the two must not be merged.

**Maharashtra gives a chronology.** Marathi devotional literature runs in three phases: an early period of about 1200-1350 with Jnaneshwar and Namdev, a dormant phase from the mid-fourteenth to the early sixteenth century, and a most brilliant third period from the early seventeenth century to the close of Peshwa rule, with Tukaram and Ramdas. Eknath's sixteenth-century revival lies between, his exact dates being given inconsistently in the standard literature.

**Verdict.** A single all-India chronology cannot contain a dormant phase, a separate institutional origin or two distinct Shaiva communities. Region, language, scripture-form and institution carry the explanation.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the Karnataka institutional cluster with Ishtalinga, Vachanas and the named assembly 6 + the Lingayat-Veerashaiva distinction on all three axes of origin, authority and varna 5 + the three-phase Marathi periodisation with at least three named saints and their dates 6 + the date-conflict handled in the correct house style 2 + a verdict that explains why a single chronology fails 1.** An answer that treats Lingayat and Veerashaiva as synonyms loses the second component in full and cannot exceed fifteen.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why can the Ishtalinga not be sorted cleanly into either the Saguna or the Nirguna column, and what does this tell you about the binary itself?

**Model answer:** Because it satisfies the defining criteria of both columns at once. The **Nirguna** school holds God to be formless and without attributes, emphasises knowledge over ritual and rejects idol worship; the **Saguna** school worships God with form and attributes, in incarnation and image. The Ishtalinga is described as **a symbol of the formless divine** - formlessness is the object of the devotion, which points to the first column - and it is **worshipped exclusively as an object**, which points to the second. The tradition that carries it, the Lingayat tradition founded by Basavanna in the twelfth century CE, is moreover usually classed as Shaiva-devotional, and it rejects elaborate ritual and caste while maintaining an exclusive devotional practice. The instruction is therefore explicit: do not force it into the binary. What this tells you about the binary is the more valuable point. Saguna and Nirguna are a **later classification applied to earlier and contemporaneous material**, useful for organising a roster and for showing that the devotional field was internally varied - but not a self-description offered by every tradition it is applied to. A classification that fits nine cases and not the tenth is still a good classification; a candidate who applies it to the tenth anyway has mistaken an analytical device for a fact.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a tradition which rejects ritual must also reject material devotional objects, or that a tradition which uses an object must hold God to have form. Those are separate commitments. The Lingayat case separates them cleanly, and the same separation runs through the whole session: rejecting an intermediary is not the same as rejecting an image, rejecting caste is not the same as rejecting hierarchy, and rejecting Sanskrit is not the same as rejecting scripture.

## Lesson 13 - Women in the Bhakti movement: two unequal routes, and the qualification every answer must carry

### Visual: the two routes, and what each cost

### 1. The learner's actual problem

This is the lesson where the temptation to write advocacy is strongest, and where advocacy costs the most marks.

The stock sentence - "the Bhakti movement gave women a voice and freed them from patriarchy" - fails on the sources' own evidence. But the opposite stock sentence - "women's participation was merely token" - fails too, because the record contains a woman of the Shudra caste with no formal education who composed over three hundred poems and is separately recorded as the oldest known female writer in her language.

What the evidence supports is neither. It supports a **structured account**: two unequal routes, a named roster across five regions and four traditions, three specific limits that can be counted, and one declared gap. That account is harder to write and far better rewarded.

### 2. The framing the sources themselves give

Women participated **"by defying social norms by leaving home for devotion or practising it within their households"**.

That is two routes, and they are **different and unequal**. Leaving home requires the capacity to leave - which, as the roster shows, correlates strongly with social position. Practising within the household means composing while performing domestic labour, often inside another person's household, and often without formal education.

Building the whole lesson on that one sentence is the correct method, because it is the sources' own analytical frame rather than an imported one, and because every name in the roster can be sorted into one route or the other.

### 3. Route A - leaving home

**Karaikkal Ammaiyar.** Tamil country; **Nayanar** (Shaiva). One of the **three women among the 63 Nayanars**. She was "a poet and devoted to Lord Shiva from childhood" and **adopted the path of asceticism** to attain her goal.

**Akka Mahadevi** (Akkamahadevi). Southern **Karnataka**; Virashaiva/Lingayat, **12th century**. A devotee of **Shiva**, called **"Akka" - elder sister - by Basavanna, Prabhu Deva, Madivalayya and Chenna Basavanna**, described as the leading philosophers of her time.

The honorific is evidence of standing, and the discipline attached to it is precise: it is standing **conferred by men**, and it should be noted as such rather than read as unqualified equality. That is not a diminishment of her position; it is an accurate description of how the position is recorded.

**Mirabai.** **Rajasthan/Mewar**; Krishna-Bhakti. From a **ruling Rajput family**, married to the son of **Rana Sanga of Mewar**. She treated **Krishna as her husband**; her poems present Krishna as yogi and lover and herself as his yogini. A well-known composition is **"Paayoji Maine Ram Ratan Dhan Paayo"**. **Guru Raidas was her preceptor.** She "renounced the world for Lord Krishna and wrote bhakti poetry and **bhajans**", and is named among the major medieval expounders of the **bhajan** with Tulsidas, Surdas and Kabir.

The qualification here is the sharpest in the lesson: **the renunciation is inseparable from her elite marital status**. The sources supply the class fact - a ruling Rajput family, a marriage into the house of Mewar - and an answer should **use** that fact rather than replace it with a generic "she defied society". A woman who leaves a ruling household is defying a *particular* set of norms from a *particular* position, and naming the position is what makes the defiance legible.

**Andal.** Tamil country; **Alvar** (Vaishnava), **8th century**. **The only female Alvar** among the canonical twelve; composed **Thiruppavai** and **Nachiyar Thirumozhi** in **pure Tamil**; her hymns form part of the **Nalayira Divya Prabandham**, the collection of 4,000 Tamil verses. Avoid the anachronistic "Meera of the South".

### 4. Route B - within the household

**Janabai.** Maharashtra; **Shudra** caste; **13th-14th century**. She worked in the household of the saint **Namdeva**, and **despite having no formal education composed over 300 poems** about her life, her daily chores and the restrictions of being a low-caste woman. She is separately recorded as **the oldest known female writer in Marathi**.

This is the **single strongest caste-and-gender case available here**, and the instruction attached to it is worth repeating: **do not lose it by listing her name only**. Everything an examiner could want is in that record - caste, region, century, the absence of formal education, a countable output, the *subject matter* of the poems, the household setting, and a language-level distinction.

**Bahina (Bahinabai).** Maharashtra; **17th century**. A poet-saint who wrote **abhangas portraying the working life of women, especially in the fields**. The content is **labour, not only devotion** - and saying so is the point. A devotional corpus whose subject is women's field labour is evidence about what devotional composition could be *used* for.

**Lal Ded.** Kashmir. A **declared gap**. She is available as a **Kashmiri example** when arguing the regional breadth of women's participation, and nothing further may be written - no composition, no date, no doctrinal position, no language detail.

### 5. The three limits, stated as counts

The qualification every women-in-Bhakti answer must carry is not a mood but three countable facts:

1. **One woman among twelve** canonical Alvars.
2. **Three among sixty-three** Nayanars - and of those three, **Isaignaniyar's inclusion is attributed to her familial connection**, as the mother of the Nayanar Sundarar, while **Mangayarkkarasiyar** is a **queen of the Pandya kingdom**. So of three women in a canon of sixty-three, one is included through a male relative and one holds royal rank.
3. The **best-attested low-caste woman's poetry survives from inside a male saint's household**.

Those three facts do a specific job. They prevent the answer from converting the **existence** of women in the canons into a claim about **equality**, while leaving the existence itself fully intact and fully creditable. The sources' own conclusion is exactly this: do **not** convert the record into a claim that devotion emancipated women, because the same record shows an inclusion resting on a male relative, a single woman among twelve, and a low-caste woman composing from within a male saint's household.

At the same time, the record contains a genuinely different case: the **Lingayat** tradition of Lesson 12 carries a **strong emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and women's participation**, with **post-puberty marriage and re-marriage encouraged**. That is a tradition-level position on women's status rather than an individual exception, and it belongs in any balanced answer as the strongest positive evidence available.

### 6. The appropriation caution

Several of these women are now claimed by later sectarian, regional and nationalist projects. A strong answer therefore:

- **distinguishes what the compositions say from what movements have since made them say**; and
- **does not present modern commemoration as medieval fact**.

This is the Lesson 2 recognition-clock applied to a person rather than to a practice, and it is the bridge to Lesson 20, where the same discipline is applied to statues, shrines, registers and listings.

### 7. The advanced framing: one roster cannot measure participation

There is a further analytical point recorded in the advanced material, and it is the correct conclusion for a high-scoring answer. **Andal is the only woman among the canonical twelve Alvars, but women also appear in other regional devotional histories** - Karaikkal Ammaiyar, Akka Mahadevi, Mirabai and Lal Ded among them - so **one roster cannot measure all devotional gender participation**.

That is a methodological claim, and it does three things at once. It concedes the limit in the Alvar canon honestly. It refuses to generalise from one canon to a whole field. And it justifies the regional breadth of the roster, which is why Lal Ded may be cited as a Kashmiri example even where nothing further about her is available.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** Women in devotional movements sit at the intersection of the culture syllabus and the society syllabus, and the boundary matters: the medieval evidence is owned here; living-community gender outcomes are owned by Indian Society.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** The **2021** Paper I demand on the nature of Bhakti literature and its contribution to Indian culture is strengthened decisively by the Janabai and Bahinabai units, because they demonstrate that devotional composition admitted authors the older literary tradition excluded. The demand is routed to Medieval History as owner. No verified demand in the audited period asks specifically about women in devotional movements; that is recorded as an honest nil rather than filled with an invented question.

**Probable framing.** "Women in the devotional movements - assess their participation and its limits"; or a demand on social inclusion where the gender evidence is the discriminating material.

**Traps.**

- Writing that devotion emancipated women.
- Writing that women's participation was merely token, when a Shudra woman with no formal education composed over 300 poems and is recorded as the oldest known female writer in Marathi.
- Dropping the **familial basis** of Isaignaniyar's inclusion, or the **royal rank** of Mangayarkkarasiyar.
- Removing Mirabai's **elite marital position** and replacing it with a generic defiance.
- Writing anything about **Lal Ded** beyond her Kashmiri attribution.
- Reading **Akka Mahadevi's** honorific as unqualified equality rather than as standing conferred by named men.
- Presenting modern commemoration of these figures as medieval fact.

### Revision notes

1. The sources' own frame: women participated **"by defying social norms by leaving home for devotion or practising it within their households"** - **two different and unequal routes**.
2. **Karaikkal Ammaiyar**: Tamil; one of the **three women among the 63 Nayanars**; a poet devoted to Shiva from childhood who **adopted the path of asceticism**.
3. **Mangayarkkarasiyar**: a **queen of the Pandya kingdom**. **Isaignaniyar**: mother of the Nayanar **Sundarar** - her inclusion is **attributed to her familial connection**.
4. **Andal**: 8th century; **the only female Alvar** among the canonical twelve; **Thiruppavai** and **Nachiyar Thirumozhi** in **pure Tamil**; part of the **Nalayira Divya Prabandham**. Avoid "Meera of the South".
5. **Akka Mahadevi**: southern Karnataka; Virashaiva/Lingayat; 12th century; devotee of Shiva; called **"Akka" - elder sister - by Basavanna, Prabhu Deva, Madivalayya and Chenna Basavanna**. Standing **conferred by men**.
6. **Mirabai**: Rajasthan/Mewar; from a **ruling Rajput family**, married to the son of **Rana Sanga**; treated **Krishna as her husband**; **"Paayoji Maine Ram Ratan Dhan Paayo"**; **Guru Raidas was her preceptor**; named among the major medieval expounders of the **bhajan** with Tulsidas, Surdas and Kabir. The renunciation is **inseparable from her elite marital status**.
7. **Janabai**: Maharashtra; **Shudra** caste; 13th-14th century; worked in the household of the saint **Namdeva**; **despite no formal education composed over 300 poems** on her life, chores and the restrictions of being a low-caste woman; **the oldest known female writer in Marathi**.
8. **Bahinabai**: Maharashtra; 17th century; **abhangas portraying the working life of women, especially in the fields** - labour, not only devotion.
9. **Lal Ded**: Kashmir - **name and region only**. A declared gap.
10. **Three countable limits**: one of twelve Alvars; three of sixty-three Nayanars, with one included through a male relative and one of royal rank; the best-attested low-caste woman's poetry composed **inside a male saint's household**.
11. The strongest positive evidence is tradition-level: the **Lingayat** emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and **women's participation**, with **post-puberty marriage and re-marriage encouraged**.
12. **Appropriation caution**: distinguish what the compositions say from what later sectarian, regional and nationalist projects have made them say; do not present modern commemoration as medieval fact.
13. **One roster cannot measure all devotional gender participation** - which is why the regional breadth of the roster matters.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Assess the participation of women in medieval devotional traditions, distinguishing what the evidence establishes from what it does not. (15 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The evidence supports a structured account rather than either a celebratory or a dismissive one.

**What it establishes.** Women participated by two routes recorded in the sources - leaving home for devotion, or practising it within the household - and both produced compositions. By the first route: Karaikkal Ammaiyar, one of three women among the sixty-three Nayanars, who adopted asceticism; Andal, the only female Alvar, whose Thiruppavai and Nachiyar Thirumozhi in pure Tamil entered the Nalayira Divya Prabandham; Akka Mahadevi of twelfth-century Karnataka, addressed as "Akka" by Basavanna and other leading figures; and Mirabai of Mewar, whose preceptor was Guru Raidas. By the second: Janabai, a Shudra woman in the household of the saint Namdeva, who despite having no formal education composed over three hundred poems on her life, chores and the restrictions of low-caste womanhood, and is recorded as the oldest known female writer in Marathi; and Bahinabai, whose abhangas portray women's field labour.

**What it does not establish.** That devotion emancipated women. One woman among twelve Alvars; three among sixty-three Nayanars, of whom Isaignaniyar is included through a male relative and Mangayarkkarasiyar holds royal rank; Mirabai's renunciation inseparable from elite marital status; Akka Mahadevi's standing conferred by named men.

**The strongest positive counterweight** is tradition-level: the Lingayat emphasis on women's participation and on re-marriage.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the two-route frame stated from the sources 3 + four named women by the first route with region and tradition 4 + two named women by the second route, with Janabai's caste, output and household stated exactly 4 + the three countable limits 3 + the Lingayat counterweight 1.** An answer that omits the counts and writes that devotion emancipated women forfeits the limits component entirely and cannot exceed nine.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is Janabai's record more valuable to an answer than Mirabai's, even though Mirabai is far better known?

**Model answer:** Because Janabai's record carries **more independent variables**, and each is separately examinable. It gives a **caste** - Shudra; a **region and century** - Maharashtra, thirteenth to fourteenth; an **educational condition** - no formal education; a **countable output** - over three hundred poems; a **subject matter** - her life, her daily chores and the restrictions of being a low-caste woman; a **setting** - the household of the saint Namdeva; and a **language-level distinction** - the oldest known female writer in Marathi. That combination is the single strongest caste-and-gender case available here, and it simultaneously supplies both halves of the argument: a low-caste woman composing at all is strong evidence of access, and composing from inside a male saint's household is the limit. Mirabai's record is culturally richer but analytically narrower for this purpose: it gives an elite origin in a ruling Rajput family, a marriage into the house of Mewar, a named preceptor in Guru Raidas, a devotional relation in which Krishna is treated as husband, a named composition and a named form. That is excellent evidence about a devotional idiom - but the class fact means her defiance cannot stand for participation generally. The strongest answers use both: Mirabai for idiom and for the class qualification, Janabai for access and for the household limit.

**Misconception to avoid:** Selecting evidence by fame rather than by what it can carry. The most celebrated figure is often the least representative, precisely because celebration tends to follow social position and later commemoration. Ask instead: how many separate things does this record establish, and does it supply its own limit? A record that supplies its own limit - a caste and a household, an honorific and who conferred it, a renunciation and a marital rank - is worth two that supply only a name and an adjective.

### 1. The learner's actual problem

Asked about Bhakti literature, most candidates produce a saint-list with no text, no language and no limit. The demand, however, is about **contribution to culture** - and a contribution has to be a change in something, stated in a way that can be checked.

The reframing is a **thesis of three transfers**, each of which is a change with a direction and a name. Once you have the three transfers, the eight units stop being a list and become evidence for a claim.

### 2. The thesis, stated in full

Bhakti's literary contribution is best measured not by devotional sentiment but by **three concrete transfers**:

1. it moved composition **out of Sanskrit into the spoken tongue**;
2. it moved authorship **outward from the Brahman scholar** - to weavers, tailors, maidservants and cultivators; and
3. it **fixed the first canonical texts** of several modern Indian literary languages -

while remaining, in its own institutional outcomes, **less egalitarian than its verses**.

That fourth clause is not a concession bolted on; it is part of the thesis, and it is what makes the thesis defensible rather than promotional.

### 3. The eight units, each as language + region + form + contribution + limit

**Unit 1 - the Alvar and Nayanar corpus.** The *Nalayira Divya Prabandham*, 4,000 verses by 12 Alvars, and the *Tevaram*, whose hymns were **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar in the seventh and eighth centuries AD** and **compiled as a canon by Nambi Andar Nambi in the late tenth or early eleventh century under Rajaraja I**. **Tamil**; Tamil country; hymn canon. **Contribution:** devotion composed in the spoken tongue, "not requiring priests, making the movement widely popular"; the Nayanars included **oil-mongers and Vellalars** as well as Brahmins and nobles. **Limit:** composed in **Tamil, not Telugu**; the composition and the compilation sit three centuries apart; and the canon was later **temple-embedded**.

**Unit 2 - Kabir's dohas.** **Hindi / Sant Bhasha**; Varanasi and the Gangetic north; couplet. **Contribution:** couplets "used by the common people of India even today" - the clearest case of devotional verse becoming everyday speech. **Limit:** transmission was **oral for generations**, and textual recensions differ.

**Unit 3 - the Adi Granth.** **Gurmukhi**, with Hindi/Braj and **Sant Bhasha**; scriptural anthology. **Contribution:** "a very composite text" carrying the dohas of **Kabir, Dadu and Nanak** and the teachings of Bhagats including **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain and Surdas**, and **two Muslim Bhagats - Kabir and Baba Farid**. **Its evidentiary value:** this single unit proves **cross-regional and cross-confessional literary circulation without asserting a merged religion** - which is precisely the standard of evidence Lesson 17 demands.

**Unit 4 - Jnaneshwar's Gita commentary.** **Marathi**; Maharashtra; commentary plus kirtan. **Contribution:** the **oldest known work in Marathi**; **credited with beginning the kirtan** in Maharashtra. **Limit:** "oldest known" is a **survival statement**.

**Unit 5 - the Abhangas of Namdev and Tukaram.** **Marathi**; Maharashtra; abhanga. **Contribution:** a named song form "in praise of the God Vithobha"; Tukaram's **Tukaramgatha** stages the **Pravritti-versus-Nivritti** conflict. **Limit:** do not date individual abhangas.

**Unit 6 - Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas.** **Awadhi**; Awadh; epic retelling. **Contribution:** made a Sanskrit epic available in a spoken dialect. **Status note:** it entered the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register in May 2024** - a **manuscript-register** fact, **not a composition date**.

**Unit 7 - Surdas's Sur Sagar and the Ashtachhap.** **Braj Bhasha**; Mathura-Vrindavan; pada and devotional song. **Contribution:** eight poets **organised in 1565 AD by Vitthalnath**, son of Vallabhacharya - four associated with Vallabhacharya and four with Vitthalnath - singing **Haveli Sangeet** within **Pushtimarg**, each assigned a part of the daily worship. **Limit:** this is a **liturgical division of labour** spanning two generations of a lineage, that is, institutionalised rather than spontaneous - and it is not a group of eight disciples of the founder.

**Unit 8 - Vachana literature.** **Kannada**; Karnataka; vachana, free-verse utterance. **Contribution:** the **Anubhava Mantapa** is recognised as **the birthplace of Vachana literature**, which "played a pivotal role in disseminating the principles of Veerashaivism". **Limit:** Basavanna's Lingayat egalitarianism **must not be projected onto Veerashaivism**, which may still acknowledge varna.

### 4. The language-growth statement, and what it does

Two further transferable contributions are recorded.

**First, Hindi.** It **"received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins"**, after which **"from the 12th century onwards, a sharp rise in regional languages such as Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati was seen."**

That is the strongest single sentence available for a language-growth demand, and it should be deployed as the pivot of any such answer, because it states the mechanism (shunning Sanskrit, and why), the date (from the twelfth century onwards) and the result (four named languages) in one movement.

It also connects backwards in a way worth noticing. Lesson 4 recorded that the Jainas' adoption of Prakrit helped that language and its literature grow, that Sauraseni gave rise to Marathi, and that the Jainas produced the earliest important works in Apabhramsa and its first grammar as well as contributing to Kannada. Lesson 5 recorded that the Buddhists' use of Pali spread their doctrine and that their later abandonment of it cut them off. **Three separate traditions, one mechanism, one kind of cultural result.** An answer that notices the pattern across all three has demonstrated something a saint-list never can.

**Second, performance.** Bhakti generated **performance genres, not only texts**: **kirtan** in Maharashtra; **Jatra** in Bengal, initiated by **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**; the revival of **Gaudiya Nritya**; **Sankirtana** in Manipur; **Abhanga**; **Bhajan**; and Assam's **Borgeet**, composed by **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva** in the **15th-16th centuries** and associated with **Ekasarana Dharma**.

The forms themselves are owned by the music, dance and theatre topics. What this session owns is the **conversion**: a devotional movement generating named genres in named regions. The Chaitanya attributions carry the sources' own hedge and must keep it.

### 5. The closing verdict, and why the qualification is part of the argument

Bhakti literature's contribution is **structural rather than sentimental** - it fixed the first canonical texts of several modern Indian languages, admitted authors the Sanskrit tradition excluded, and produced performance forms still in use. But the same movement's **institutional afterlife** - sects, sampradayas, endowed shrines and later state commemoration - **reattached that literature to authority**, which is why its egalitarian claims should be **assessed in the verses rather than assumed from the movement**.

That final clause is the single most useful methodological instruction in this whole block. "Assess in the verses rather than assume from the movement" is a testable standard: it tells you where to look, and it tells you what would count as evidence either way.

### UPSC use

**Syllabus relevance.** This is the point where the culture syllabus's literature and language dimension and its religion dimension are the same question.

**Verified PYQ linkage.** The **2021** Mains Paper I demand - **"Evaluate the nature of the Bhakti literature and its contribution to Indian culture"**, 10 marks, 150 words - is answered directly from this lesson, and it is routed to Medieval History as owner. The directive **"evaluate"** requires a judgment with a weighing, which is exactly what the three transfers plus the institutional-afterlife qualification supply. At 150 words, the executable route is: thesis of three transfers, then four or five of the eight units compressed to language + form + contribution, then the Hindi and regional-language sentence, then the qualification. The **2018** demand on the **re-orientation under Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**, 15 marks and 250 words, takes its performance evidence from the same material with the hedge intact.

**Probable framing.** "Assess the contribution of devotional literature to the growth of regional languages"; "Devotional movements produced forms as well as texts - discuss".

**Traps.**

- Producing a saint-list with no text, no language and no limit.
- Using the 2024 register inscription of the Ramcharitmanas as a composition date.
- Attributing the Ashtachhap's organisation to spontaneity rather than to a **liturgical assignment** made in **1565 AD by Vitthalnath**, or calling its eight poets Vallabhacharya's own disciples.
- Projecting Lingayat egalitarianism onto **Veerashaivism**.
- Dating individual abhangas, or forgetting that "oldest known work in Marathi" is a **survival statement**.
- Dropping the **hedge** on Chaitanya's performance attributions.
- Asserting doctrinal merger from the Adi Granth's inclusion of two Muslim Bhagats - it proves **circulation**, not merger.

### Revision notes

1. **Three transfers**: out of **Sanskrit into the spoken tongue**; **outward from the Brahman scholar** as author; into the **first canonical texts** of several modern Indian literary languages - while the institutional outcomes remained **less egalitarian than the verses**.
2. **Unit 1**: *Nalayira Divya Prabandham* (4,000 verses, 12 Alvars) and the *Tevaram* - hymns **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar, 7th-8th centuries AD**, **compiled by Nambi Andar Nambi, late 10th-early 11th century, under Rajaraja I**; **Tamil**; the Nayanars included **oil-mongers and Vellalars**. Not Telugu.
3. **Unit 2**: **Kabir's dohas** in Hindi and **Sant Bhasha** - "used by the common people of India even today"; transmission **oral for generations**, recensions differ.
4. **Unit 3**: the **Adi Granth** in **Gurmukhi** - "a very composite text" with dohas of **Kabir, Dadu, Nanak** and Bhagats including **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain, Surdas**, and **two Muslim Bhagats, Kabir and Baba Farid**. Proves **cross-regional and cross-confessional circulation**, not merger.
5. **Unit 4**: **Jnaneshwar's** Marathi **Gita commentary**; the **oldest known work in Marathi**; **credited with beginning the kirtan**.
6. **Unit 5**: **Abhangas** of **Namdev and Tukaram**, "in praise of the God Vithobha"; the **Tukaramgatha** stages **Pravritti versus Nivritti**.
7. **Unit 6**: **Tulsidas's Ramcharitmanas** in **Awadhi**; entered the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register, May 2024** - a register fact, not a composition date.
8. **Unit 7**: **Surdas's Sur Sagar** in **Braj Bhasha**; the **Ashtachhap**, eight poets **organised in 1565 AD by Vitthalnath** - four associated with Vallabhacharya, four with Vitthalnath - **Haveli Sangeet**, each assigned a part of the daily worship.
9. **Unit 8**: **Vachana** literature in **Kannada**; the **Anubhava Mantapa** its recognised birthplace; it disseminated the principles of **Veerashaivism** - do not project Lingayat egalitarianism onto it.
10. **The language sentence**: Hindi "received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins"; **from the 12th century onwards a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati**.
11. **Performance output**: **kirtan** (Maharashtra), **Jatra** (Bengal, Chaitanya), revived **Gaudiya Nritya**, **Sankirtana** (Manipur), **Abhanga**, **Bhajan**, and Assam's **Borgeet** by **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva**, 15th-16th centuries, associated with **Ekasarana Dharma**.
12. **Verdict**: structural rather than sentimental; but sects, sampradayas, endowed shrines and later state commemoration **reattached** the literature to authority - so assess the egalitarian claims **in the verses**, not from the movement.
13. The same language mechanism appears in **three** traditions: Jaina Prakrit and Ardhamagadhi, Buddhist Pali, and devotional vernaculars. Naming the pattern is worth more than naming any one instance.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Devotional composition changed Indian languages more than it changed Indian society." Discuss. (20 marks, 250 words)**

#### Model

The proposition is largely sustainable, and the comparison is the point.

**The linguistic change is measurable.** Composition moved out of Sanskrit into the spoken tongue: Tamil in the Alvar and Nayanar canons, Marathi in Jnaneshwar's Gita commentary and in the abhangas of Namdev and Tukaram, Awadhi in the Ramcharitmanas, Braj Bhasha in the Sur Sagar, Kannada in the Vachanas, Gurmukhi and Sant Bhasha in the Adi Granth. Hindi is recorded as having received its biggest boost from the movement, which shunned Sanskrit as the language of the Brahmins, and a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati is dated from the twelfth century onwards. Several of these corpora became their languages' earliest canonical texts. Devotion also generated performance genres - kirtan, Jatra, Sankirtana, Borgeet by Sankardeva and Madhavdeva.

**The social change is harder to demonstrate.** Authorship did widen: the Nayanars included oil-mongers and Vellalars, and Janabai, a Shudra woman without formal education, composed over three hundred poems from inside a male saint's household. But early Tamil devotion became temple-embedded and could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers; the Ashtachhap that Vitthalnath organised in 1565 was a liturgical division of labour; and sects, sampradayas and endowed shrines reattached the literature to authority.

**Verdict.** The linguistic transformation is structural and traceable; the social transformation is real at the level of authorship and partial at the level of institutions.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = six named language-and-text pairings 6 + the recorded Hindi and regional-language statement with its date and mechanism 4 + the performance genres with at least three named forms and one named composer pair 3 + the authorship widening with two named cases 4 + the institutional-reattachment counter-evidence with two named instances 3.** An answer that argues the social claim without the temple-embedding and sampradaya evidence cannot exceed twelve.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Adi Granth contains compositions by two Muslim Bhagats. What exactly does this prove, and what does it not?

**Model answer:** It proves **circulation**, and it does not prove **merger**. What the record establishes: the Adi Granth is "a very composite text", written in **Gurmukhi**, carrying the dohas of **Kabir, Dadu and Nanak** and the teachings of Bhagats including **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain and Surdas**, among whom **Kabir and Baba Farid** are the two **Muslim Bhagats**. Since Namdev is a Marathi saint and Baba Farid propagated mainly in Punjab, the anthology demonstrates **cross-regional and cross-confessional literary circulation** - compositions travelling across language, region and confessional boundary and being preserved together in one scripture. That is a **text** fact, verifiable, dateable and locatable. What it does **not** establish is that the traditions involved merged doctrinally, that their adherents held a common theology, or that a composite religion existed. Inclusion in an anthology is an editorial and devotional act by the compiling tradition; it tells you what that tradition valued and preserved, not what the included authors believed about each other's doctrines. The precise sentence to write: "The Adi Granth's inclusion of Kabir and Baba Farid among its Bhagats is direct evidence of cross-confessional literary circulation in a single scriptural anthology - and it is evidence about circulation, not about merged belief."

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating shared preservation as shared doctrine. The same error recurs in every part of this topic - shared shrine treated as shared faith, shared song form treated as shared theology, shared vocabulary treated as shared metaphysics. The discipline is always the same: name what kind of thing the evidence is (a text, a place, a practice, a person), and then state only the claim that kind of evidence can support. Lesson 17 makes this the organising principle of the whole synthesis argument.
