# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

Four blocks, each drawing across several lessons. These are retrieval-and-integration checks rather than recall prompts: every one requires material from at least three lessons.

## Block A - the method (Lessons 1-3)

**Question:** A single sentence reads: "Indian philosophy, which began with the Upanishads around 600 BCE, produced six spiritual schools whose teachings are preserved in the Samkhya Sutra of Kapila and the Yogasutra of Patanjali, written in the second century BCE." Sort every claim into the six fields and the six clocks, and say which survive.

**Model answer:** Seven claims are packed in, and only three survive intact. **Survives - a text-clock claim:** the **Upanishads were compiled around 600 BCE**, in the land of the **Panchalas and Videha**, towards the end of the Vedic period. **Survives - a doctrine claim:** there are **six orthodox darshanas** - Samkhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa and Vedanta. **Survives with correction - a classification claim:** they are **not "spiritual schools"** but schools of inquiry classified by their acceptance of the **Vedas as supreme revealed scripture**, a criterion about textual authority rather than about theism; Samkhya, inside the six, does not recognise the existence of God. **Fails - "began with":** the Upanishadic turn is a reaction against **priestly domination and ritual** within an existing tradition, not an origin point for philosophy. **Fails - authorship:** the extant **Samkhya Sutra** is **late**, so Kapila's association is traditional and must not be reported as settled authorship. **Fails - dating:** the **Yogasutra** is **layered and of debated date**, commonly placed in the early centuries BCE or CE; "written in the second century BCE" supplies a precision the evidence does not carry. **Fails - "preserved in":** two texts cannot preserve the teachings of six schools. The correctly sorted version keeps the text-clock date, the classification with its criterion, and the school names, and declines the authorship and the year.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a sentence which is mostly right is usable. In this topic a sentence is only as strong as its weakest claim, because an examiner reading "the Samkhya Sutra of Kapila, written in the second century BCE" now has two reasons to distrust everything else in the paragraph. Sorting by field and clock before writing is faster than repairing afterwards.

## Block B - the shramana formations and the darshana frame (Lessons 4-8)

**Question:** Buddhism and Jainism arose in the same region, in the same century, against the same background, and ended very differently. Explain the divergence using three variables taught in this session, and then say what each tradition contributed to Indian languages.

**Model answer:** **Variable one - the social doctrine.** Buddhism **attacked the varna system** and took people into the order **without consideration of caste**, admitting women to the sangha; Jainism **did not condemn varna**, explaining birth-position by past action while holding liberation open to the lower castes. **Variable two - the institutional base and its economics.** Buddhism won large state patronage, was carried by **Asoka** into Central Asia, West Asia and Sri Lanka, and accumulated wealth - from the first century AD large-scale image worship brought rich offerings, royal grants followed, **Nalanda drew revenue from as many as 200 villages**, and by the seventh century establishments were dominated by ease-loving people; that wealth made monasteries targets, and monks were killed at Nalanda while others escaped to Nepal and Tibet. Jainism **did not win as much state patronage** and spread more slowly, but **retains its hold** where it spread. **Variable three - language.** Buddhism used **Pali, the language of the people**, and later **gave up Pali for Sanskrit**, cutting the monks off from the mainstream of people's life; Jainism discarded Sanskrit for **Prakrit**, wrote in **Ardhamagadhi**, and compiled its texts at **Valabhi in the sixth century AD**. **The language contributions:** Jaina adoption of Prakrit helped that language grow, **Sauraseni** gave rise to **Marathi**, the Jainas produced the **earliest important works in Apabhramsa and its first grammar**, wrote epics, Puranas, novels and drama, later used Sanskrit extensively, and contributed substantially to **Kannada**. Buddhism's contribution was the circulation of doctrine in **Pali**, and its reversal is one of the recorded causes of its decline.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining Buddhism's disappearance by invasion alone. Invasion is the fifth of five recorded causes, and it operates on a tradition already weakened by the language reversal, the wealth of the monasteries, the reform of its rival and the confinement of its deepest offer to the monks. An answer that names one cause has explained a tenth of the problem.

## Block C - the devotional field (Lessons 9-14)

**Question:** Construct the evidence chain that takes a devotional movement's four causes through to a measurable effect on an Indian language, naming every link.

**Model answer:** **Link one - the causes.** Four are recorded: reaction against **Brahmanical orthodoxy and caste rigidity**; promotion of **equality and inclusion** for marginalised communities; **stagnation of Buddhism and Jainism** leaving space for devotional religion; and **royal patronage**, named causally, exemplified by **Chola support for the Shaivite movement**. **Link two - the mechanism.** Devotion was preached as the path to salvation and composed **primarily in Tamil rather than Sanskrit**, which is recorded as **not requiring priests, making the movement widely popular** - a gatekeeping barrier removed. **Link three - the canons.** The **Nalayira Divya Prabandham**, 4,000 Tamil verses by 12 Alvars, and the **Tevaram**, whose hymns were **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar in the seventh and eighth centuries AD** and **compiled by Nambi Andar Nambi under Rajaraja I** roughly three centuries later; the Nayanars included **oil-mongers and Vellalars** alongside Brahmins and nobles. **Link four - regional recurrence rather than diffusion.** Kannada **Vachana** literature at the **Anubhava Mantapa**; the Marathi sequence of **Jnaneshwar 1275-96**, **Namdev 1270-1350**, a sixteenth-century revival and **Tukaram 1598-1650**; Awadhi in the **Ramcharitmanas**; Braj Bhasha in the **Sur Sagar**; Gurmukhi and **Sant Bhasha** in the **Adi Granth**. **Link five - the measurable effect.** Hindi "received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins", and **from the twelfth century onwards a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati** is recorded. **Link six - the limit.** Early Tamil devotion became **temple-embedded** and could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers; sects, sampradayas, endowed shrines and later state commemoration reattached the literature to authority.

**Misconception to avoid:** Stopping at link five. The chain without its limit is advocacy, and in this material the limit is drawn from the same sources as the claim - which is what makes stating it a strength rather than a hedge.

## Block D - interaction, material culture and the present (Lessons 15-20)

**Question:** You are given four pieces of evidence: a Sufi practice, a scriptural anthology, a Delhi procession and a Nandi mandapa. State precisely what claim each can support, and then state one claim that none of them can support.

**Model answer:** **The practice - sama.** Spiritual dance and music, **expressly noted as not Islamic**, supports a claim of **documented practice-level absorption** by a tradition from outside itself. It supports nothing about doctrine, because the same evidence records that Sufi ideological structures **remained Islamic**. **The anthology - the Adi Granth.** "A very composite text" in **Gurmukhi**, carrying the dohas of **Kabir, Dadu and Nanak** and Bhagats including **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain and Surdas**, with **Kabir and Baba Farid** as two **Muslim Bhagats**, supports a claim of **cross-regional and cross-confessional literary circulation** - a Marathi Varkari saint and a Punjab Chishti preserved in one scripture. It supports nothing about merged belief, because inclusion is an editorial act by the compiling tradition. **The procession - Phool Walon Ki Sair.** Floral fans carried from **Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki's tomb** to the **Yogmaya Temple**, **patronised by Akbar II**, **banned by the British**, **reinstated in 1962**, supports a claim about **sponsorship, interruption and restoration** - that shared observance depends on who holds authority. It does not support unbroken continuity, which the prohibition explicitly contradicts. **The architecture - a Nandi mandapa.** The mount built as a separate structure **placed just before the sanctum sanctorum**, at **Brihadishvara, Lepakshi, Ellora Kailasa, Ramappa** and the **Hoysala group**, supports a claim of **doctrine converting into a design decision**. It supports nothing about a plan derived from a philosophical text, which no source records. **What none of them supports:** that Indian religious traditions merged into a composite faith. Practice, text, place and architecture each evidence a crossing at their own level; the doctrinal and institutional levels show the opposite, from the **Naqshbandi** counter-current to an elite project that **never had mass acceptance**.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that four kinds of evidence pointing the same way add up to a stronger claim of the same kind. They do not; they add up to four separate, well-supported claims at four different levels. The synthesis argument is won by stating the level, not by accumulating instances.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six original questions, none reproducing or paraphrasing an official demand. Word ceilings are those of the General Studies Paper I: 10 marks in 150 words, 15 and 20 marks in 250 words. Every model observes its ceiling.

## Original 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Distinguish between the removal of an intermediary and the abolition of a hierarchy in Indian religious history, using two named examples of each.**

### Model

The two are different claims and require different evidence.

**Removal of an intermediary** means that a religious end becomes attainable without a specialist. The eightfold path was taught so that a follower **would not depend on the machinations of the priests**; and Tamil devotional composition in the spoken tongue is recorded as **not requiring priests, making the movement widely popular**. Both are mechanisms, and both are demonstrable.

**Abolition of a hierarchy** means that social ranking ceases to operate. The evidence is far weaker. The Buddhist order excluded **debtors and slaves**, and the escape it offered was confined to the monks; early Tamil devotion became **temple-embedded** and could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers.

The strongest positive case is tradition-level rather than individual: the **Lingayat** emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and women's participation.

Access changed demonstrably; hierarchy changed unevenly, and the two must not be merged.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the conceptual distinction stated before any example 2 + two named intermediary-removal cases with their recorded wording 4 + two named hierarchy limits 3 + the Lingayat counter-case 1.** An answer that treats the two claims as one cannot exceed three, because the entire demand is the distinction.

## Original 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Explain, with three named examples, why the language in which a religious tradition composed is a cultural decision rather than an accident.**

### Model

Language choice determines who can receive a teaching without an intermediary, which makes it a decision with social consequences.

**First, Jainism.** The early Jainas **discarded Sanskrit**, patronised mainly by the brahmanas, and **adopted Prakrit**, writing in **Ardhamagadhi** and compiling at **Valabhi** in the sixth century AD. The consequence was the growth of Prakrit, of **Sauraseni** from which Marathi grew, the **earliest works in Apabhramsa** with its first grammar, and a contribution to **Kannada**.

**Second, Buddhism.** The use of **Pali, the language of the people**, spread the doctrine; the monks' later adoption of **Sanskrit** cut them off from the mainstream of people's life and is a recorded cause of decline.

**Third, devotional composition.** Hindi "received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins", and from the twelfth century a sharp rise in **Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati** followed.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the access mechanism stated as the governing principle 2 + three traditions, each with its chosen language named 3 + at least four downstream literary or linguistic consequences 4 + the reversal case showing the mechanism running backwards 1.** An answer that names the languages without a single downstream consequence is capped at four.

## Original 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"An institution is the price a religious movement pays for its reach." Examine this proposition with reference to Indian religious history.**

### Model

The proposition captures a pattern that recurs across traditions with different doctrines, which is what makes it more than a metaphor.

**The pattern in Buddhism.** The sangha's open doors and the use of Pali gave the teaching reach; the same institution accumulated offerings from the first century AD, royal grants, and revenue - Nalanda drawing on as many as 200 villages - until by the seventh century establishments were dominated by ease-loving people, and that wealth attracted plunder.

**The pattern in devotional movements.** Composition in the spoken tongue removed the priestly intermediary and gave it popularity; but early Tamil devotion became temple-embedded and could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers, the Ashtachhap that Vitthalnath organised in 1565 was a liturgical division of labour, and sects, sampradayas and endowed shrines reattached devotional literature to authority.

**The pattern in Sufism.** Khanqahs gave the orders presence and reach; inam land grants institutionalised the cult of the shaikh, and the shrines' main function remained that of a religious institution.

**The counter-instance.** Advaita's four mathas at Dwaraka, Puri, Sringeri and Badrikashrama are an institution built deliberately to distribute a teaching geographically, where reach was the purpose rather than the by-product.

**Verdict.** The price is real but is better described as a transformation than as a loss: institutionalisation converts a message into an endowment, a lineage and a managerial class - which is why every claim about a movement's egalitarianism must be tested separately at the level of its verses and at the level of its institutions.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = the proposition restated as a testable pattern 2 + three traditions, each showing reach and its institutional cost with named evidence 9 + one counter-instance where the institution was the design rather than the price 2 + a verdict that reframes "price" as transformation and states the two levels of testing 2.** An answer that illustrates the pattern in only one tradition cannot exceed six.

## Original 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Assess the value and the limits of the Saguna-Nirguna classification for understanding medieval Indian devotion.**

### Model

The classification is analytically valuable and is not a self-description of everything it is applied to.

**Its value.** It states a genuine doctrinal division. The Nirguna school held God to be **formless and without attributes**, emphasised knowledge over ritual, rejected scripture and idol worship, opposed Brahmin supremacy, valued personal encounters with God, and blended Vaishnava Bhakti, Nathpanthi and Sufi traditions. The Saguna school worshipped God **with form and attributes**, in incarnation and image, in two groups - Ram-Bhakti and Krishna-Bhakti. The division has philosophical backing: a position admitting attributes in the absolute gives image worship a warrant, which is why Ramanuja belongs to the Saguna school.

**Its first limit - awkward cases.** The Lingayat **Ishtalinga** is a symbol of the **formless divine**, worshipped exclusively as an object, inside a tradition classed as Shaiva-devotional. It satisfies both criteria and must not be forced into either column.

**Its second limit - unresolved descriptions.** Ramananda is called an **Advaita scholar** while being placed in the **Saguna** school; the two descriptions are not reconciled in the reference literature and should be reported rather than tidied.

**Its third limit - chronology.** It is a later classification applied to earlier material, including the Tamil canons.

**Verdict.** Use it to organise the roster and to demonstrate internal variety; do not use it as though every tradition had chosen a side.

**Unique scoring note:** **15 marks = both schools stated with their full recorded positions 5 + the philosophical alignment that justifies the classification 3 + three distinct limits, each with a named case 6 + a verdict distinguishing an organising device from a historical self-description 1.** An answer that defines the two schools and stops has demonstrated recall of the classification but not the assessment the demand requires, and is capped at five.

## Original 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Trace how political authority has acted upon India's religious traditions from the ancient period to the present, and assess what its repertoire of actions has grown to include.**

### Model

Authority has acted on religion continuously, but the *kinds* of action available to it have multiplied, and that expansion is the real subject.

**Sponsorship, the oldest action.** Kshatriya princes in Panchala and Videha cultivated the reforming atmosphere in which the Upanishads were compiled. Kharavela patronised the Jainas in Kalinga in the first century BCE; Karnataka's basadis received royal land grants from the sixth century. Asoka despatched agents to Central Asia, West Asia and Sri Lanka; Kanishka patronised Mahayana and convened a council in Kashmir.

**Commissioning and canon-making.** Rajaraja I's reign saw Nambi Andar Nambi compile the Tevaram - authority producing not a building but a canon. Iltutmish titled Bahauddin Zakariya Sheikh-ul-Islam; inam land grants institutionalised the cult of the shaikh; Chola support for the Shaivite tradition is recorded as causal.

**Prohibition, then restoration.** The Delhi procession between Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki's tomb and the Yogmaya Temple was patronised by Akbar II, banned by the British, and reinstated in 1962.

**Classification.** The newest action is administrative: a culture ministry nominating through a national academy to an intergovernmental committee, which inscribes a practice under a named domain at a dated session.

**Assessment.** The repertoire has grown from endowing to commissioning, prohibiting, restoring and finally classifying. What it has never determined is doctrine.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = four distinct kinds of action, each named as an action rather than as a period 8 + at least six named patrons, officials or instruments spread across those actions 6 + the canon-making and classification actions, which a purely architectural account would miss 4 + a closing assessment stating what the repertoire has never reached 2.** An answer that narrates patronage chronologically without distinguishing the kinds of action cannot exceed ten, because the demand is about the repertoire, not the sequence.

## Original 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Evaluate the proposition that India's religious traditions are better described as interacting than as syncretic.**

### Model

The proposition is well founded because the two words make different claims and the evidence supports only one of them cleanly.

**What "interacting" can be shown.** Four kinds of evidence, each named. **Practice**: sama, expressly noted as not Islamic; qawwali, ghazal and Baul Gaan, whose lyrics carry both Hindu Bhakti and Suphi influence and are exemplified by the songs of Kabir. **Text**: the Adi Granth carrying Kabir and Baba Farid as its two Muslim Bhagats alongside a Marathi Varkari saint and a Braj poet; Sheikh Hamiduddin of Nagaur translating many Persian books into Hindi. **Place**: the Urs at Ajmer, and the Delhi procession between Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki's tomb and the Yogmaya Temple. **Person**: Amir Khusrau; and Pir Budhan Shah, venerated across three communities, in discourse with Guru Nanak.

**What "syncretic" would additionally require, and lacks.** Evidence of combination into a single system. Against it stand the record that Sufi ideological structures remained Islamic; the Naqshbandi order, radical in nature, whose leading saint styled himself Mujaddid and which counted Aurangzeb among its followers; an elite imperial project that never had mass acceptance; and, on the devotional side, opposition to Buddhism and Jainism as a major theme of the Tamil hymns.

**One qualified exception.** The Nirguna school is itself recorded as blending Vaishnava Bhakti, Nathpanthi and Sufi traditions - a statement about one devotional stream's composition, not about two religions.

**Verdict.** Interaction without identity-erasure, with one stream-level exception honestly stated.

**Unique scoring note:** **20 marks = the vocabulary distinction between interaction and syncretism stated as the governing move 3 + four kinds of evidence with at least six named instances 8 + four counter-instances at doctrinal or institutional level 6 + the one qualified stream-level exception, correctly bounded 2 + a verdict naming the level at which each claim holds 1.** An answer that accumulates instances of sharing without a single counter-instance cannot exceed ten, because it has argued only one side of a demand that asks for an evaluation.

# REMEDIATION

Five error sets, each with the error, the correction and the evidence that settles it, followed by three drills.

## Error set A - terminology and category

| Error | Correction |
|---|---|
| "The Bhakti sect" / "the Sufi sect" | Both are **movements**, analytical labels for families of regional developments carried by **lineages** and **orders**. A sect divides by discipline or authority and has a bounded community |
| "Orthodox schools were theistic" | The criterion is acceptance of the **Vedas as supreme revealed scripture**. Samkhya, inside the six, **does not recognise the existence of God** |
| "The six darshanas were rival religions" | Six schools of **inquiry** inside one tradition, sharing the Purushartha frame |
| "Lingayat and Veerashaiva are the same" | The traditional Veerashaiva community is said to **pre-date Basavanna**, traces its origin to the **Panchacharyas**, **accepts some Vedic tradition** and **may still acknowledge varna** |
| "Vishishtadvaita is part Advaita, part Dvaita" | It is **qualified monism** in its own right: Brahman **with attributes**, devotion as the means |
| "Sufi sources say..." | Name the genre - **malfuzat**, **maktubat** or **tazkira** - and a work: *Munis al-Arwah*, *Awarif al-Ma'arif*, *Kashf al-Mahjub* |
| "Nayanars and Alvars composed in Tamil and Telugu" | Their canonical hymn traditions are **Tamil**; Telugu devotion has separate later histories |

## Error set B - chronology and attribution

| Error | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Kapila wrote the *Samkhya Sutra*" | The association is **traditional**; the extant text is **late** |
| "The *Yogasutra* was written in [year]" | **Layered, debated date**, commonly placed in the early centuries BCE or CE |
| "Eknath (1533-99)" or "Eknath, born 1518" as *the* date | The same reference work gives **both**; write **sixteenth century** and the contribution; report both only for a chronological demand |
| "Pushtimarg was founded by Madhvacharya" | **Vallabhacharya, c. 1500 AD**. Madhvacharya founded the **Brahma Sampradaya** |
| "The *Bijak* is Dadu Dayal's" | A recorded trap; the attribution is to **Kabir** |
| "Bhakti began in the south in the seventh century and reached the north in the fifteenth" | Tamil traditions are **early major formations**; other regions have **different chronologies**; no single diffusion date |
| "Chandragupta Maurya's conversion proves Jainism's early spread to Karnataka" | A **late tradition, not corroborated by any other source**; **epigraphic evidence is not earlier than the third century AD** |
| "The Nayanars were Chola-period saints, as the Darasuram relief shows" | The relief dates the **sculpture**, not the saints |
| "Ramcharitmanas, inscribed in 2024" used as a composition date | A **manuscript-register** fact: the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, Ulaanbaatar, **May 2024** |

## Error set C - mechanism and over-claim

| Error | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Bhakti abolished caste" | The movement's **appeal** to marginalised communities is a recorded cause; its **institutional outcome** was temple-embedding that could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers |
| "Bhakti emancipated women" | **One** of twelve Alvars; **three** of sixty-three Nayanars, one included through a male relative and one of royal rank; the best-attested low-caste woman's poetry composed **inside a male saint's household** |
| "The Sufis preached love and tolerance" | Orders disagreed on **property, state proximity and initiation practice**; the **Naqshbandi** order was **radical in nature** with **Aurangzeb** among its followers |
| "Wahdat-al-wujud proves borrowing from Vedanta" | A **resemblance of doctrine-language** noted in the reference literature; no transmitting text, person, date or direction is recorded |
| "Ellora shows planned religious tolerance" | **Continuing sanctity and competitive patronage** - 34 caves, 12 Buddhist, 17 Hindu, 5 Jain |
| "The temple plan derives from Vedanta" | A **bounded correspondence**, to be flagged; **no source records a derivation** |
| "The Adi Granth proves a composite religion" | It proves **cross-regional and cross-confessional circulation**; inclusion is an editorial act by the compiling tradition |
| "A heritage listing proves the festival is ancient and authentic" | A **recognition-clock** fact about an administrative decision at a dated session |

## Error set D - omission

| Omitted item | Why it costs marks |
|---|---|
| **Royal patronage** among the four causes of the devotional movement | It is named as **causal, not incidental**; omitting it makes the movement look self-propelled |
| **Opposition to Buddhism and Jainism** in the Tamil hymns | It is **one of the major themes**, and its omission produces a harmony narrative the sources contradict |
| The **temple-embedding** complication | Without it, the inclusion claim is advocacy |
| The **Salim Chishti** counter-case to the court-distance rule | The same order that began at a distance from power later stood at its centre |
| The **pre-Buddhist** origin of stupa-making | It is the strongest counter to "Buddhism invented the stupa" |
| **Debtors and slaves** excluded from the sangha | Without it, "open to all" is an over-claim |
| The **Ashtachhap** as a liturgical assignment made by **Vitthalnath in 1565 AD**, spanning two generations | Turns a poetic circle into institutionalised devotion, and blocks the "eight disciples of Vallabhacharya" compression |
| The **inam** land grants | The economic base that institutionalised the cult of the shaikh |

## Error set E - the declared gaps

| Temptation | The correct response |
|---|---|
| Supplying **Lal Ded's** dates, verses or doctrine | Cite her as a **Kashmiri example only**; nothing further exists in this framework |
| Writing an **Al-Biruni account of Somnath**, or a dated consecration | Neither exists here; support statement 1 and the Al-Biruni identity, and hand the temple's political history to Medieval History |
| Supplying **Vastu or Shilpa-shastra** rules, chapters or measurements | None exist here; this is **the most likely fabrication point** in the philosophy-and-monuments question |
| Reconstructing the derivation chain among the **older Buddhist schools** | Owned by the ancient-religion topic; this framework states the classification and stops |
| Writing detailed **Nyaya or Vaisheshika** doctrine | Carried here **at classification level only**; the evidence used secures them no further |
| Describing a **Syrian Christian church or liturgy** | A declared bounded absence |
| Giving **population figures, decline rates or legal status** for a minority community | This framework holds none; the "rapidly shrinking" phrase is a **characterisation**, to be attributed |
| Inventing a **Dadu Dayal** biography | The evidence gives little beyond the naming |
| Inventing a **Dvaita salvation formula** to complete a table | Give the **sampradaya chain** instead - Madhvacharya, Brahma Sampradaya, Gaudiya Vaishnavism, ISKCON |

## Drill sets

**Drill 1 - the clock drill.** For each of the following, name the clock and the single sentence it licenses: (i) the *Tevaram* hymns composed in the seventh and eighth centuries AD; (ii) the *Tevaram* compiled by Nambi Andar Nambi under Rajaraja I; (iii) Moinuddin Chishti, 1143-1236; (iv) the Ashtachhap established in 1565 AD; (v) the Nayanar legends carved at Airavatesvara; (vi) the Sufi movement in India beginning around 1300 AD; (vii) Deepavali inscribed in December 2025. *Target: seven correct clock assignments - note that (i) and (ii) concern the same text on two different clocks - and seven sentences that do not transfer across clocks.*

**Drill 2 - the field drill.** Take any five sentences from a newspaper or a standard textbook about Indian religion and sort each clause into text, person, doctrine, institution, practice-and-place or status. *Target: every clause assigned; any clause that cannot be assigned is a value claim and should be struck.*

**Drill 3 - the mechanism-and-limit drill.** For each of the following claims, supply one named mechanism and one named limit, from this session only: (i) devotion widened religious access; (ii) Sufi practice absorbed local elements; (iii) philosophy shaped Indian monuments; (iv) India's smaller religious communities demonstrate composite formation; (v) medieval traditions survive into the present. *Target: ten named items, none of them an adjective.*

## Map 3 - mechanism against limit, five cases

| Case | Named mechanism of inclusion | Named documented limit |
|---|---|---|
| Buddhist sangha | Doors open irrespective of caste and sex; the eightfold path removing dependence on priests; Pali | Debtors and slaves excluded; escape confined to the monks; lay followers taught to come to terms with the existing situation |
| Tamil devotion | Composition in the spoken tongue, "not requiring priests"; oil-mongers and Vellalars in the canon | Temple-embedding that could strengthen rulers and Brahman managers; opposition to Buddhism and Jainism as a major theme |
| Women in devotion | Two recorded routes; Janabai composing over 300 poems without formal education; the Lingayat emphasis on women's participation | One of twelve Alvars; three of sixty-three Nayanars, one by a male relative, one of royal rank; the best low-caste corpus from inside a male saint's household |
| Sufi orders | Sama; shared song forms; translation; shared shrine observance; service to humanity | Ideological structures remained Islamic; the Naqshbandi counter-current; inam grants institutionalising the cult of the shaikh; shrines' main function religious |
| An imperial religious project | An interfaith debate forum; imperial patronage | It never had mass acceptance |

```text
               Bhagats; Persian->Hindi     a general harmony
               translation                 asserted without a
     PLACE     Ajmer Urs; Phool Walon      named practice, place
               Ki Sair                     or text
     PERSON    Amir Khusrau; Pir Budhan    an influence chain
               Shah + Guru Nanak           built from RESEMBLANCE
     SCHOOL    the NIRGUNA school blends   an inference from the
               Vaishnava Bhakti +          ABSENCE of recorded
               Nathpanthi + Sufi           conflict
         |
     COUNTER-EVIDENCE THAT MUST BE STATED
     -> Sufi ideological structures REMAINED ISLAMIC
     -> NAQSHBANDI: radical; Sirhindi = Mujaddid; Aurangzeb
     -> INAM grants institutionalised the cult of the shaikh
     -> the shrines' MAIN FUNCTION was to be a religious institution
     -> an elite imperial project NEVER HAD MASS ACCEPTANCE
     -> TAMIL HYMNS: opposition to Buddhism and Jainism = major theme
         |
     VERDICT: INTERACTION WITHOUT IDENTITY-ERASURE
     practices, texts, places and persons crossed boundaries that
     doctrines and institutions kept
```

## Map 6 - master comparison of the eight recurring distinctions

| # | Distinction | The discriminating fact |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Nayanar / Alvar | Shiva and **63** against Vishnu and **12**; *Tevaram* against *Nalayira Divya Prabandham* |
| 2 | Saguna / Nirguna | God **with** form and attributes against God **formless and without attributes**; the Ishtalinga fits neither cleanly |
| 3 | Lingayat / Veerashaiva | Basavanna and the Vachanas against a community said to **pre-date** him, tracing origin to the **Panchacharyas**, accepting some Vedic tradition and possibly acknowledging varna |
| 4 | Advaita / Vishishtadvaita | **Nirguna** Brahman reached by **knowledge** against Brahman **with attributes** reached by **devotion** |
| 5 | Chishti / Suhrawardi | Distance from power and Chishti initiation practice against **property, knowledge and hal**, ritual observance and state proximity |
| 6 | Ba-shara / Be-shara | In favour of Sharia against against it - **Qalandars, Madaris, Malangs, Haidaris**, who left almost no written accounts |
| 7 | Jainism / Buddhism | Gods below the jina and varna **not condemned** against **no god or atman** and varna **attacked**; Prakrit against Pali; survival against disappearance |
| 8 | Circulation / merger | An anthology, a translation, a shared shrine and a shared song form against a claim about combined doctrine, which none of them carries |

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

Compressed recall notes covering every lesson. Organised by the topic's own structure rather than by a generic template.

## The apparatus: six fields, six clocks, one prohibition

- Six fields for any claim: **text, person, doctrine, institution, practice-and-place, status**. **Value** is a seventh column and earns nothing.
- Six clocks: **text-layer, person, institution, monument phase, practice, recognition**. Mixing two in one sentence is an unsupported claim.
- Prohibition: never open in the value column; never transfer a recognition date backwards; never build an influence chain from resemblance.
- House style for a conflicted fact: state the **century**, the **contribution** and the **conflict**; never choose a year.

## Purushartha and the orthodox/heterodox split

- **Artha** (Arthashastra), **Dharma** (Dharmasastra), **Kama** (Kamasutra), **Moksha** (darshana texts). A tradition licensing four ends produces an art showing four ends.
- **Orthodox** = accepts the Vedas as supreme revealed scripture: **Samkhya, Yoga, Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa, Vedanta**. The criterion is textual authority, **not theism**.
- **Heterodox**: **Charvaka / Lokayata** - the materialistic view, rejecting the supernatural, God, Brahman and salvation, recognising only **fire, earth, water and air**; what is not experienced through the senses does not exist. India developed **both idealist and materialist** systems.

## The late Vedic reaction and the Upanishadic turn

- Sacrificial religion as a cultural system: specialists, liturgical language distinct from speech, patron-client economy, result-oriented logic; **Panini's Ashtadhyayi**, 4th century BCE, is its grammatical by-product.
- Reaction against **priestly domination and ritual**, especially among the **Panchalas and Videha**, where the **Upanishads were compiled around 600 BCE**.
- Four moves: criticise ritual; stress right belief and **knowledge**; know the **atman** and its relation to **Brahma**; **Brahma** emerges as the supreme god.
- **Kshatriya princes participated** - patronage, at the earliest stage.
- The social reading - changelessness serving stability, the atman-Brahma relation serving loyalty - is an **analytical reading**, to be attributed.

## Jainism as a cultural formation

- **Mahavira**: b. **540 BCE** near Vaisali (Basarh); kshatriya father, Lichchhavi mother, Magadhan connections; renounced at **30**; wandered **12 years**; **kaivalya at 42**; preached **30 years**; d. at **72** in **468 BCE** at **Pavapuri**.
- **Five doctrines**: no violence, no lying, no stealing, no property, **brahmacharya** - the fifth Mahavira's addition to four from previous teachers, **Parsva** named. **Three gems**: full knowledge, action, liberation. **No ritual needed** for liberation.
- Gods **recognised but below the jina**; varna **not condemned**; birth-position explained by past action; **lower castes can attain liberation**; human values sought even in a *chandala*.
- Order admitted **both men and women**; followers recorded as **14,000**.
- Language: **Prakrit**, literature in **Ardhamagadhi**, compiled **6th century AD at Valabhi, Gujarat**. Consequences: Prakrit's growth; **Sauraseni -> Marathi**; **earliest Apabhramsa works and its first grammar**; epics, Puranas, novels, drama; later Sanskrit; substantial **Kannada**.
- Division: a **late, uncorroborated** tradition of Chandragupta Maurya's conversion; a **12-year famine**; migration south under **Bhadrabahu**; the **Pataliputra council boycotted** by the southerners - thereafter **Digambara** (south) and **Svetambara** (Magadha).
- Institutions: epigraphic evidence in Karnataka **not earlier than the 3rd century AD**; **basadis** from the **6th century** with royal land grants; **Kalinga from the 4th century BCE**, **Kharavela** in the **1st century BCE**; southern Tamil Nadu 2nd-1st centuries BCE; later Malwa, Gujarat, Rajasthan.
- Less state patronage, slower spread, but **retains its hold**.

## Buddhism as a cultural machine, and its five failure points

- **Buddha**: b. **563 BCE**, Sakya kshatriya, **Kapilavastu**; left home at **29**; knowledge at **35** at **Bodh Gaya**; first sermons at **Sarnath**; preached **40 years**; d. at **80** in **483 BCE** at **Kusinagar**.
- **Eightfold path** - right observation, determination, speech, action, livelihood, exercise, memory, meditation - attributed in a text of c. **3rd century BCE**; its mechanism is that a follower **does not depend on the machinations of the priests**.
- **Middle path**: avoid the excess of **both luxury and austerity**. **Code**: do not covet, commit violence, use intoxicants, lie, or indulge in corrupt practices.
- **No god and no atman**; **varna attacked**; entry without consideration of caste; **women admitted** to the sangha.
- Limits from the same record: **debtors and slaves excluded**; escape **confined to the monks**; monks resembled brahmanas in living on alms and upholding a class order, differing in basing varna on **action and attributes** rather than **birth**.
- **Buddha, sangha, dhamma**; vows of **continence, poverty, faith**. **Pali** spread it; **Asoka** made it a world religion via agents to Central Asia, West Asia and Sri Lanka.
- **Mahayana** - the Great Wheel - arises in a contact setting: **image worship**, open doors; others called **Hinayana**; **Kanishka** its patron, convening a **council in Kashmir** whose copper-engraved teachings lie under an **undiscovered stupa**; **Gandhara** Graeco-Roman Buddha images; **Mathura** (Buddha images, the headless Kanishka statue, stone images of Mahavira); **Nagarjunakonda and Amaravati**.
- **Five causes of decline**: reform-to-ritual; the reformed rival assuring **women and sudras admission to heaven**; **Pali abandoned for Sanskrit**; the monastery economy (**Nalanda** on as many as **200 villages**; ease-loving domination by the **7th century**; the later **Vajrayana** form); plunder by **Turkish invaders**, monks escaping to **Nepal and Tibet**. Practically extinct by the **12th century AD**.
- Abiding mark: argument over acceptance; non-violence and cattle protection, **Suttanipata** calling cattle *annada vannada sukhada*; an economic diagnosis of poverty.

## Sect vocabulary

- **School** divides by a position; **sect** by discipline or authority within a tradition; **order** by a rule of life and transmission chain; **sampradaya** by founder + doctrine + lineage; **movement** by nothing formal.
- A sect claim must give **what divides, who is inside, and one institution or text that carries it**.
- **Sthanakvasi** is **Jaina**. **Mahasanghikas** associated with Mahayana's development; the finer derivation chain among older schools is a **declared limit** here.
- **Ajivikas** enter as a patronage-and-architecture fact: **Barabar and Nagarjuni** under **Asoka and Dasharatha**.
- Sampradayas named: **Ramanandi** (largest monastic group within Hinduism; Rama; **Tyagi** and **Naga**; disciples **Dhanna** and **Pipa**), **Brahma** (Madhvacharya), **Dashanami** (Shankaracharya), **Pushtimarg** (Vallabhacharya, c. 1500 AD).

## The darshanas at cultural depth

- **Samkhya**: **purusha / prakriti**; three means of knowledge - **pratyaksha, anumana, shabda**; liberation through **real knowledge**; **does not recognise the existence of God**; traditionally associated with **Kapila**, the extant sutra **late**.
- **Yoga**: the **Yogasutra**, layered and of debated date; control of **citta-vritti**; **eight-limbed practice**; a **portable discipline**. Do not reduce every yoga tradition to a slogan.
- **Nyaya and Vaisheshika**: carried here at **classification level only** - a declared limit.
- **Mimamsa**: salvation through **performance of Vedic ritual with proper understanding**; **Sutras of Jaimini**, **Sabar Swami**, **Kumarila Bhatta**; recorded as having **legitimised social divisions and reinforced Brahman dominance** - an assessment.
- The triad of means: **performance, knowledge, devotion**.

## Vedanta and its sub-schools

- **Vedanta** = "Veda" + "anta"; upholds the philosophies elaborated in the **Upanishads**; oldest foundational text the **Brahmasutra of Badarayana**; **Brahman is the reality**, else **maya**; **atman similar to Brahman**; supports **karma and punarjanma**; liberation only through realising Brahman. **Prasthanatrayi** = Upanishads + Gita + Brahma Sutras.
- **Advaita**: **Shankaracharya, 8th century AD**, probably from **Kerala**; **oldest extant** sub-school; **nirguna** Brahman; **jnana** the means; **Jivanmukti**; **Dashanami Sampradaya** and **four mathas / Amnaya Peethas** - **Dwaraka (W), Puri (E), Sringeri (S), Badrikashrama (N)**; **Upadesasahasri**. The local table row is **partly clipped** - attribute nothing beyond this.
- **Vishishtadvaita**: **Ramanuja, 11th-12th century**; Brahman **with attributes**, directly against Shankaracharya; **love and devotion**, not jnana alone; therefore in the **Saguna** school; influenced **Vishnuvardhana's** conversion. Not "part Advaita, part Dvaita".
- **Dvaita**: **Madhvacharya**; **Brahma Sampradaya**; Vishnu as **Para-Brahma**; **Dvaita Vedanta** and **Sadh Vaishnavism**; **Gaudiya Vaishnavism** via **Chaitanya**; **ISKCON**. The evidence gives a **lineage**, not a separate soteriology.
- **Pushtimarg** is **Vallabhacharya's**, c. **1500 AD** - not Madhvacharya's.

## Why devotion happened, and why there was no relay

- **Four causes**: orthodoxy and **caste rigidity**; **inclusion** of marginalised communities; **stagnation of Buddhism and Jainism**; **royal patronage** - **Chola support for the Shaivite movement**, named as **causal**.
- Spread of the devotional cult **from the 7th century AD**, especially in the south; devotion as offering and **prasada** with complete surrender; the tenant-and-landowner comparison is an **analytical reading**.
- **Tantric** texts, shrines and practices from about the **6th century AD**, admitting **women and sudras**, permeating **Jainism, Buddhism, Saivism and Vaishnavism** - a **separate** development.
- No single diffusion date: Tamil traditions are **early major formations**; **Virashaiva, Marathi, Kannada, Bengali, Assamese, North Indian sant and Vaishnava** traditions follow different chronologies.
- The complication: early Tamil devotion became **temple-embedded** and could **strengthen rulers and Brahman managers even while opening devotion beyond Vedic scholarship**.
- Method: state a **specific mechanism** and a **specific limit** together.

## The Tamil canons

- **Nayanars** = Shiva, **63**; **Alvars** = Vishnu, **12**. **Tevaram** hymns **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar, 7th-8th centuries AD**; **compiled by Nambi Andar Nambi, late 10th-early 11th century, under Rajaraja I**; **Nalayira Divya Prabandham** = **4,000 Tamil verses**.
- **Andal**, 8th century, **the only woman among the twelve**; **Thiruppavai** and **Nachiyar Thirumozhi** in **pure Tamil**. Never "Meera of the South".
- Three women among the 63: **Karaikkal Ammaiyar**, **Mangayarkkarasiyar** (a Pandya queen), **Isaignaniyar** (mother of Sundarar).
- Composition **primarily in Tamil, not Sanskrit**; recorded as **not requiring priests, making the movement widely popular**. Canon included **oil-mongers and Vellalars** as well as Brahmins and nobles.
- **They explicitly opposed Buddhist and Jain asceticism**; opposition was **one of the major themes** - defining, not incidental. A "pure harmony" account **misdescribes the sources**.
- Transmission shaped by later **temple compilation and Brahmanical and royal institutions**; the **63 Nayanar legends** appear in the sculptural programme of the **Airavatesvara Temple at Darasuram**.
- Never attribute the canons to **Telugu**.

## The Saguna and Nirguna rosters

- **Nirguna**: God **formless, without attributes**; **knowledge over rituals**; rejects scripture and idol worship; opposes Brahmin supremacy; values personal encounters; **blends Vaishnava Bhakti, Nathpanthi and Sufi traditions**.
- **Kabir**: Varanasi; Hindi and **Sant Bhasha**; **dohas** "used by the common people of India even today"; **disciple of Ramananda**, later founder of the separate **Kabirpanthi** sect; one of two **Muslim Bhagats** in the **Adi Granth** with **Baba Farid**; the *Suphi* strand in **Baul Sangeet**. The *Bijak* is not Dadu Dayal's.
- **Guru Nanak**: dohas in the **Adi Granth**, "a very composite text" in **Gurmukhi**; **Sant Bhasha** draws on **Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khariboli, Persian**.
- **Dadu Dayal**: named; dohas in the Adi Granth; **no biography may be invented**.
- **Ravidas**: Varanasi; a **Bhagat** in the Adi Granth; **preceptor of Mirabai**.
- **Saguna**: God **with form and attributes**, in incarnation and image; **two groups - Ram-Bhakti and Krishna-Bhakti**.
- **Ramanuja**: Saguna; Vishishtadvaita; devotion as the means; the cultural consequence is a philosophy legitimising image worship and temple devotion.
- **Ramananda**: called an **Advaita scholar** yet placed in the **Saguna** school - **report both**; **Ramanandi Sampradaya**, largest monastic group within Hinduism; **Tyagi** and **Naga**; **Dhanna** and **Pipa**.
- **Chaitanya Mahaprabhu**: Bengal; **Gaudiya Vaishnavism**; Brahma Sampradaya -> **ISKCON**; **"it is said"** he revived **Gaudiya Nritya** and initiated **Jatra** - keep the hedge.
- **Tulsidas**: Awadh; **Awadhi**; **Ramcharitmanas**; plus **Vinaya Patrika, Krishna Gitavali, Kavitavali, Gitavali, Sahitya Ratna, Dohavali, Vairagya Sandipani**; **Ramanandi** sect.
- **Surdas**: Braj; **Braj Bhasha**; **Sur Sagar**; best known of the **Ashtachhap**, eight poets **organised in 1565 AD by Vitthalnath**, son of Vallabhacharya - four associated with Vallabhacharya, four with Vitthalnath - **Haveli Sangeet**, **each assigned a part of the daily worship**.

## Karnataka and Maharashtra

- **Basavanna**, **12th century CE**: **Lingayat** tradition; exclusive worship of the **Ishtalinga**, a symbol of the **formless divine**; egalitarian society; rejection of caste and elaborate ritual; **women's participation**; **post-puberty marriage and re-marriage encouraged**; scripture the **Vachanas**; the **Anubhava Mantapa** at **Basavakalyan, Bidar district**, "the world's inaugural spiritual assembly", led by **Allama Prabhu**, **the birthplace of Vachana literature**.
- **Veerashaiva** is **not** Lingayat: said to **pre-date Basavanna**; origin traced to the **Panchacharyas - Renuka, Daruka, Ghantakarna, Dhenukarna, Vishvakarna**; **accepts some Vedic tradition**; **may still acknowledge varna**.
- The **Ishtalinga** must not be forced into the Saguna/Nirguna binary.
- Marathi periodisation: **c. 1200-1350**; **dormant** mid-14th to early 16th century; **most brilliant** early 17th century to the close of Peshwa rule.
- **Jnaneshwar 1275-96**: **oldest known work in Marathi**; **credited with beginning the kirtan**; **Gita commentary in Marathi**; **Ringan** and **Dhava** in the sect's pilgrimage.
- **Namdev 1270-1350**: many **Abhangas**; a **Bhagat** in the **Adi Granth**; **Abhanga** praises **Vithobha**, composed and sung by Tukaram and Namdev.
- **Eknath**, **sixteenth century**: "the seed of literary genius germinated again"; **dates conflicted and unresolved**.
- **Tukaram 1598-1650**: "the greatest poet in Marathi literature"; asceticism, toleration, devotion; **Tukaramgatha**; **Pravritti versus Nivritti**. **Sant Tukaram Shila Mandir, Dehu, inaugurated 2022**.
- **Ramdas**, 17th century: **Dasbodh**. Political readings route away.

## Women in devotion

- Two routes: **leaving home** / **practising within the household** - different and unequal.
- **Karaikkal Ammaiyar** (Tamil, Nayanar, asceticism); **Andal** (only female Alvar); **Akka Mahadevi** (Karnataka, 12th century, called **"Akka"** by **Basavanna, Prabhu Deva, Madivalayya, Chenna Basavanna** - standing conferred by men); **Mirabai** (Mewar; **ruling Rajput family**; married to the son of **Rana Sanga**; Krishna as husband; **"Paayoji Maine Ram Ratan Dhan Paayo"**; **Guru Raidas** her preceptor; a major **bhajan** expounder with Tulsidas, Surdas, Kabir).
- **Janabai** (Maharashtra; **Shudra**; 13th-14th century; household of **Namdeva**; **no formal education**, **over 300 poems** on her life, chores and low-caste womanhood; **oldest known female writer in Marathi**). **Bahinabai** (17th century; **abhangas on women's field labour**).
- **Lal Ded**: **Kashmir - name and region only**.
- Three limits: **1 of 12**; **3 of 63**, one by a **male relative**, one of **royal rank**; the best low-caste corpus **from inside a male saint's household**.
- Counterweight: the **Lingayat** tradition-level emphasis on equality, anti-caste positions and women's participation.
- **Appropriation caution**: separate what compositions say from what later sectarian, regional and nationalist projects make them say.

## The literature and its measurable effect

- **Three transfers**: out of **Sanskrit**; outward from the **Brahman author**; into the **first canonical texts** of several modern languages - while institutional outcomes stayed **less egalitarian than the verses**.
- Eight units: **Tamil canons**; **Kabir's dohas**; the **Adi Granth**; **Jnaneshwar's Gita commentary and kirtan**; the **Abhangas** and **Tukaramgatha**; **Ramcharitmanas**; **Sur Sagar** and the **Ashtachhap**; **Vachana** literature.
- **Hindi** "received its biggest boost with the Bhakti movement, which shunned the use of Sanskrit as it was the language of the Brahmins"; **from the 12th century a sharp rise in Bengali, Hindi, Marathi and Gujarati**.
- **Performance**: **kirtan**, **Jatra**, **Gaudiya Nritya**, **Sankirtana**, **Abhanga**, **Bhajan**, **Borgeet** by **Sankardeva and Madhavdeva**, 15th-16th centuries, associated with **Ekasarana Dharma**.
- Verdict: **structural rather than sentimental**; assess the egalitarian claims **in the verses**, not from the movement.

## Sufism

- **Tasawwuf**; from **suf** (wool) or **safa** (purity); India **from c. 1300 AD**; South India by the **15th century**; orders = **silsilahs**; **Ain-i-Akbari** reported as listing **fourteen**; **Ba-shara** / **Be-shara** (**Qalandars, Madaris, Malangs, Haidaris** - almost no written accounts).
- Stages: **Khanqah (10th c.)**, **Tariqa (11th-14th c.)**, **Tarifa (15th c. onwards)**.
- Vocabulary: **fana, insan-e-kamil, zikr/dhikr, wahdat-al-wujud, sama** (**not Islamic**), **muraqabah, ziyarat, pir/shaikh, murid, khalifa, muqaddam, wilaya, tasfiyat al-qulub**.
- **Chishti**: order from **Chisht** near Herat, **930 AD**; **Moinuddin 1143-1236**, **Ajmer**, **Gharib Nawaz**, annual **Urs**; **Jahanara Begum's Munis al-Arwah**; philosophy on **wahdat-al-wujud**, noted as similar to Vedantic language. **Nizamuddin Auliya** = **Mahbub-e-Ilahi**, **sultan-ul-mashaikh**, **renunciation and service to humanity**; **Amir Khusrau** his most famous disciple, credited with **Qawwali**, **Ghazal, Tarana, Trivat**; **Ain-i-Hind** bestowed on Sirajuddin Usmani. Others: **Bakhtiyar Kaki** (Qutub Minar begun in his memory), **Baba Farid** (Punjab; Adi Granth), **Chiraag-e-Dilli**, **Sultan-e-Tarkeen** (Persian into Hindi), **Burhanuddin Gharib** (South India), **Salim Chishti** (Akbar).
- **Court-distance rule**: spiritual authority extended while **political sovereignty was leased to kings**. **Salim Chishti is the counter-case.**
- **Suhrawardi**: **Bahauddin Zakariya 1182-1262**, **Sheikh-ul-Islam** under **Iltutmish**, **Multan**; **property, knowledge and hal**; **mysticism + ilm**; Punjab, Sindh, Kashmir, parts of Bengal; zenith under **Ruknuddin 1251-1335**; **rejected Chishti initiation practice** and accepted **property and state proximity**.
- **Qadiriyya**: **Shah Nimatullah**; Punjab, especially Sindh; **Miyan Mir** enrolled **Dara Shikoh**; **Hasrat Mohani**, **Muhammad Iqbal**.
- **Naqshbandi**: **Baha-ud-Din Naqshband**; popular under **Akbar**; **Ahmad Sirhindi = Mujaddid**; **radical**; **Aurangzeb** among its followers.
- **Shattari**: **Sirajuddin Abdullah Shattar**; Malwa, Jaunpur, Bengal; **Tansen**.
- **Kubrawiya / Firdausia**: **Najm-ad-Din Kubra**; eastern India, Bangladesh, Mauritius; **no major expansion**.
- Economy: **inam land grants** **institutionalised the cult of the shaikh**; the shrines' **main function was to be a religious institution**.
- Genres: **malfuzat**, **maktubat**, **tazkira**; works - ***Munis al-Arwah***, ***Awarif al-Ma'arif***, ***Kashf al-Mahjub***.

## The Sikh material, bounded

- **Guru Nanak** among the three key **Nirguna** figures. **Sikh doctrinal and institutional history is not owned here.**
- **Adi Granth**: "a very composite text", **Gurmukhi**; dohas of **Kabir, Dadu, Nanak**; Bhagats **Ramananda, Namdev, Ravidas, Parmanand, Sain, Surdas**; two **Muslim Bhagats - Kabir and Baba Farid**.
- **Sant Bhasha**: **Punjabi, Apabhramsa, Hindi, Braj Bhasha, Sanskrit, Khariboli, Persian**.
- **Pir Budhan Shah**, venerated by Sikhs, Muslims and Hindus, in **spiritual discourse with Guru Nanak at Rawalpindi** - no date supplied.
- The anthology proves **circulation**, not **merger**.

## Interaction and its limits

- Controlling statement: **borrowing of practices and shared devotional spaces** while **Sufi ideological structures remained Islamic** - **interaction without identity-erasure**.
- Four kinds of evidence: **practice, text, place, person**. Resemblance of abstract formulations is **not** evidence.
- The five-part spine: **sama / qawwali / ghazal / Baul Gaan**; **Kabir and Baba Farid in the Adi Granth** and **Hamiduddin's** Persian-to-Hindi translations; **Ajmer's Urs** and **Phool Walon Ki Sair**; **Amir Khusrau** and **Pir Budhan Shah**; and **the limit**.
- **Phool Walon Ki Sair**: floral fans from **Khwaja Bakhtiyar Kaki's tomb** to the **Yogmaya Temple**; **Akbar II** in the 19th century; **banned by the British**; **reinstated in 1962 by Jawaharlal Nehru**.
- Limits: **Naqshbandi**; **inam**; the shrines' main function; an elite project that **never had mass acceptance**; and the Tamil hymns' **opposition** theme.
- Vocabulary: prefer **interaction, borrowing, sharing, circulation, translation, co-location**.

## Doctrine made buildable

- **Purushartha -> Khajuraho** breadth of life - a **reading**; the patrons left no statement.
- **Aniconism -> symbol**: empty throne, Bodhi tree, wheel, stupa, footprint; **Bharhut**, early **Sanchi**. The later image marks a change in **devotional practice**.
- **Relic cult -> stupa**: **anda, harmika, chattra, vedika, pradakshinapatha**; **circumambulation, not entry**; **Sanchi's Great Stupa**; **stupa-making is pre-Buddhist**.
- **Withdrawal -> excavated architecture**: **viharas** and **chaityas**; **Barabar and Nagarjuni** for the **Ajivikas** under **Asoka and Dasharatha**; **Ellora**; **Udayagiri-Khandagiri**. Royal endowment adds **political display**.
- **Vahana -> separate structure "just before the sanctum sanctorum"**: **Brihadishvara, Lepakshi, Ellora Kailasa, Ramappa**, the **Hoysala group**.
- **Bhakti -> enclosure, corridor, tank, gopuram**: **Belur** after **Vishnuvardhana's** conversion under **Ramanujacharya**; the **Nayaka prakaram** and graded gopuras; the **Nayanar legends at Airavatesvara**.
- **Vedanta -> garbhagriha**: a **bounded correspondence**, flagged; **no derivation is recorded**.
- **Cosmology -> orientation**: the **Sarnath** capital's four directions. Do not generalise.
- **Shared space**: **Ellora's 34 caves - 12 Buddhist, 17 Hindu, 5 Jain**; **Khajuraho's Hindu and Jain temples in one idiom** - **continuing sanctity and competitive patronage**, not a designed interfaith project.
- Counter-weight: material and technique, dynastic legitimation, guild and merchant finance, site and geology. Verdict: **programme, vocabulary, scale**.
- Prohibitions: no derivation from a text; no intention attributed to a silent patron; no interfaith reading of Ellora; **no Vastu or Shilpa-shastra rules, chapters or measurements**.

## Festivals, pilgrimage and the smaller communities

- Four types with anchors: **pilgrimage-congregation** (**Kumbh Mela** at **Prayagraj, Haridwar, Ujjain, Nashik** - sacred geography, **temporary urbanism**, sanitation, crowd and safeguarding management); **agricultural/seasonal** (**Pongal, Bihu, Onam, Baisakhi**, Hornbill-linked calendars - ecology, harvest, regional identity); **ritual-performance** (**Ramlila, Ramman, Mudiyettu, Durga Puja, Garba** - music, dance, theatre, artisan labour, transmission); **multi-community** (**Deepavali** - participation varies; never one uniform meaning).
- Festivals make synthesis **observable but not automatic**; shared participation may coexist with region-specific meanings, exclusion, commercialisation and environmental pressure.
- **Parsi**: **Atash Behram / Atash Adaran / Atash Dadgah**; **8 Atash Behrams**, **100+ Dadgahs**, mostly **Maharashtra and Gujarat**; **plain exterior** because the point is the **sacred fire and Yasna ceremony**, not glorification; inner sanctum; smoke vents. **Avesta**, **Yashts**, **Khordeh Avesta**, **Denkard** (10th c. AD, **no divine status**), **Bundahishn**, **Qissa-i Sanjan** (fire temple at **Sanjan, Gujarat**). **Yasna** by **Dasturs**; **16 types of fire**. **Ahura Mazda** against **Angra Mainyu**; **Zarathustra**, c. **7th century BC**; India **8th-10th century AD**; **Parsis and Iranis**. **Parsi Theatre 1850s-1920s** -> **film production from the 1930s**.
- **Jewish**: **Magen David (Kolkata)**, **Paradesi (Cochin)**, **Ohel David / Lal Deval (Pune)** - **largest in India**. **Torah** (first five books of the **Tanakh**), **Talmud**, **Halakhah**; **Eliyahu-hanavi**; **tsisith**. Five communities **by language**: **Cochin Jews** (Malayalam), **Bene Israel** (Marathi, **the largest**), **Baghdadi Jews** (traders; **Surat**, then Mumbai and Kolkata), **Bnei Menashe** (**Manipur and Mizoram**; **Mizo, Kuki, Chin**), **Bene Ephraim** (Telugu; converted in the **1990s**).
- **St Thomas Christians**: **as per the community's own written records**, Christianity introduced by **Thomas the Apostle**, **Malabar, 52 AD**. **Bounded**: no church building, no distinct liturgy described here. **Kerala Murals** attributed to contact with **Greeks, Romans and Persians**.
- The argument: formation through **migration, trade and refuge**; **language as marker** - a religion retained, a language adopted.
- Cautions: not static survivals; do not aggregate; **no population figures, decline rates or legal status**.

## The modern layer and the answer spine

- Modern commemoration is evidence about the **act of commemorating** - who, under what instrument, when.
- **Dehu 2022**; a very large seated **Ramanuja** statue inaugurated at **Hyderabad**; **Ramcharitmanas** on the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, **Ulaanbaatar, May 2024**, with the **Panchatantra** and **Sahrdayaloka-Locana**; **Deepavali** on the **ICH Representative List**, **20th session, Red Fort, New Delhi, 8-13 December 2025**, India's **16th element**, nominated by the **Ministry of Culture** through the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**, in **social practices, rituals and festive events**; **ISKCON** in the **Brahma Sampradaya**.
- **Pluralism boundary**: constitutional provisions, comparative secularism, contemporary policy and living-community outcomes are **handed back**; this framework supplies the evidential substrate and the method.
- **The spine**: claim-type -> mechanism -> exact evidence -> right clock -> limit from the same base -> evidence grade -> qualified verdict and hand-off.

## Bounded-research declaration

The substantive base for this session is the complete Core owner, the complete Advanced owner and one core OCR-searchable local source. External and live research was confined to three declared purposes and stopped when each was resolved.

**(a) Exact question verification and routing.** Official Mains General Studies Paper I papers for 2018 to 2025 and Preliminary General Studies Paper I papers for 2018 to 2026 were read from the locally held text of the official scans to confirm exact wording, response choices, marks and word limits, and the routing ledgers were consulted to confirm ownership and serial numbers. The 2026 Mains General Studies Paper I routing ledger was read in full to establish that **no question in that paper is routed to this topic** - recorded here as an honest nil rather than filled. A subsequent verification pass added two routed-away Preliminary items that the teaching already referenced but the index had omitted: the **2023 Dhanyakataka** item and the **2024 Upanishads** item, both now reproduced exactly and answer-neutrally.

**(b) A bounded material-fact pass**, conducted in two stages against a pre-declared list. Every item is a chronology, attribution or institutional-status claim whose mis-statement would have created a real defect, and each was closed as soon as it was resolved.

*First stage - two items, both status claims:*

1. *The current living-heritage record used in Lesson 19.* Resolved: Deepavali was inscribed on the Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity at the **twentieth session** of the Intergovernmental Committee, held at the **Red Fort, New Delhi, 8-13 December 2025**; it is recorded as India's **sixteenth element**; the nomination was prepared by the **Ministry of Culture** through the **Sangeet Natak Akademi**; the domain of inscription is **social practices, rituals and festive events**.
2. *The register on which Ramcharitmanas was inscribed in 2024.* Resolved, and it materially refines the owner's wording: the inscription was on the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, at the regional committee's **tenth general meeting in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia, in May 2024**, together with the *Panchatantra* and *Sahrdayaloka-Locana*.

*Second stage - three items raised by independent audit, all attribution or count claims where the topic's own spine proved unreliable:*

3. *The Tevaram's composers and its compiler.* Resolved: hymns **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar in the seventh and eighth centuries AD**; canon **compiled by Nambi Andar Nambi in the late tenth or early eleventh century, under Rajaraja I**.
4. *The formation of the Ashtachhap.* Resolved: **organised in 1565 AD by Vitthalnath**, son of Vallabhacharya, comprising **four poets associated with Vallabhacharya** and **four with Vitthalnath**.
5. *The number of silsilahs recorded in the Ain-i-Akbari.* Resolved as **fourteen**, and written as a reported figure rather than as a direct reading of the original.

Each of the five is logged in the discrepancies table below with the wording it replaced. The pass was then closed; no further live source was consulted and no optional corroboration was sought.

**(c) One brief current linkage.** Exactly one current record appears in this file, in Lesson 19, and is cross-referenced in Lesson 20. Every other lesson's pre-teach checklist records truthfully that no current linkage was required or found.

**Qdrant** was not used. It is an optional fallback under the governing source order, and the Markdown owners, the OCR source and the locally held official papers resolved every requirement without it. Its non-use did not delay or reduce any part of this session.

## Discrepancies recorded rather than resolved

| # | Point | The disagreement | How this file handles it |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Eknath's lifespan** | The Core owner reports that the same reference work gives "Eknath (1533-99)" in one place and states that he was born in 1518 in another | Reported as an **open and unresolved** conflict; "sixteenth-century Marathi saint of the Varkari tradition" is used throughout; both dates are shown only where the demand is explicitly chronological; no chronology, contemporaneity or ordering argument rests on it (L2 s2, L12 s7) |
| 2 | **Kapila's date** | The OCR source gives Kapila a birth around 580 BCE and attributes the Samkhya system to him; the Core owner's control states that the extant *Samkhya Sutra* is late and that Kapila must not be treated as its settled author | The **attribution caution is followed**; the school's content is taught and the date is not adopted. The difference between the two sources is recorded here rather than silently resolved (L2 s2, L7 s3) |
| 3 | **Ramananda's school** | The Core owner records that the reference literature calls Ramananda an *Advaita* scholar while placing him in the **Saguna** school | **Both are reported**, and the tension is taught as a source-discipline case rather than tidied away (L11 s4, and its concept check) |
| 4 | **The register for Ramcharitmanas** | The Core owner says "UNESCO's Memory of the World register in 2024"; the bounded material-fact pass establishes the **Memory of the World Asia-Pacific Regional Register**, Ulaanbaatar, **May 2024** | The **more exact formulation is used**, and the owner's looser wording is recorded here as the point refined (L11 s4, L14 s3, L20 s3) |
| 5 | **The Advaita table row** | The Core owner records that the local table row for Advaita is **partly clipped** in the held source | The clipping is **declared in the lesson**, and nothing beyond the quoted material is attributed to that row (L8 s3) |
| 6 | **Four darshanas' verification level** | The Advanced owner records that Nyaya, Vaisheshika, Mimamsa and Vedanta were confirmed **only at the classification level**, not through a full paragraph-level re-extraction | Mimamsa and Vedanta are taught from **other securely held passages** in the Core owner; **Nyaya and Vaisheshika are carried at classification level only**, with the limit declared to the learner (L7 s3) |
| 7 | **The 2020 Mains routing pointer** | The controlling Mains routing ledger points the 2020 philosophy-and-monuments demand at the Advanced owner; the Core owner records that Core routing supersedes that pointer and that Core is the owner of record | The demand is treated as **owned by this topic**, executed from the Core bank, and the supersession is recorded (L18; PYQ index) |
| 8 | **Local text of a 2020 objective item** | The locally held OCR text of the 2020 Preliminary item on Buddhist schools **drops the sect name** in its second statement | The item is **not reproduced verbatim**; it appears in the routed-away table under a neutral rendering, with the incompleteness declared (PYQ index) |
| 9 | **Serial number of a 2026 objective item** | The locally held official 2026 Preliminary paper and the audited 2026 routing ledger do not agree on the serial number of the Jain forms-of-existence item | **No serial number is asserted** for that item anywhere in this file; it is recorded by year, paper and demand only, under the serial-number policy stated at the head of the PYQ index (PYQ index; Coverage Matrix D) |
| 10 | **The Tevaram's composers and its compiler** | The reference literature used as the topic's spine compresses two distinct acts, naming **Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar** as compiling the *Tevaram* in the **tenth century AD** - that is, it assigns the composers a compilation date three centuries after their own period | The bounded material-fact pass establishes the standard position: the hymns were **composed by Appar, Sambandar and Sundarar in the seventh and eighth centuries AD**, and were **compiled as a canon by Nambi Andar Nambi in the late tenth or early eleventh century, under Rajaraja I**. The corrected form is used throughout, the two acts are placed on different clocks, and the compression is recorded here as a source-owner error rather than silently overwritten (L2 s3; L10 s2 and s6; L14 unit 1; Maps 1, 2 and 4) |
| 11 | **The composition of the Ashtachhap** | The same literature describes the Ashtachhap as the **eight poet-disciples of Vallabhacharya**, established in **1565 AD** with the foundation of Pushtimarg - compressing a two-generation group into one teacher's circle and merging two institutional dates | The bounded pass establishes that the group was **organised in 1565 AD by Vitthalnath**, Vallabhacharya's son, and comprises **four poets associated with Vallabhacharya** (Surdas, Kumbhandas, Paramanandadas, Krishnadas) and **four with Vitthalnath** (Govindswami, Chhitswami, Chaturbhujdas, Nandadas). The corrected form is used throughout, and the Pushtimarg foundation of c. 1500 AD is kept separate from the 1565 organisation (L2 s3; L11 s4; L14 unit 7) |
| 12 | **The number of silsilahs in the Ain-i-Akbari** | The topic's spine reports the *Ain-i-Akbari* as mentioning "a dozen" silsilahs | The bounded pass finds the standard reported figure to be **fourteen**, but **only secondary compilations were available and no direct reading of Abu'l Fazl's text was made**. The text therefore states that the work is *reported as listing* fourteen, attributing the count rather than asserting it. This is the weakest-graded of the five live checks, and the qualification travels with every occurrence, including the scoring-note component that depends on it (L15 s2; L15 revision notes and Mains model; register notes) |

## Declared gaps - material a candidate may expect that this session does not supply

1. **Lal Ded** beyond her Kashmiri attribution - no dates, verses, language details or doctrinal position exist in this framework.
2. **Any Al-Biruni account of the Somnath temple**, and any ritual definition or dated consecration of that temple.
3. **Vastu or Shilpa-shastra rules, chapter names or measurements** - none exist in this framework, and supplying them is the most likely fabrication point in the philosophy-and-monuments demand.
4. **The derivation chain among the older Buddhist schools** - owned by the ancient-religion topic; the classification is stated and the chain is not reconstructed.
5. **Detailed Nyaya and Vaisheshika doctrine** - carried at classification level only, on the Advanced owner's own verification note.
6. **The Jain classification of the four principal forms of existence** - the subject of a 2026 objective item routed to Ancient History; the method is supplied and the classification is not reconstructed.
7. **Any Syrian Christian church building or liturgical practice** - a bounded absence in the Core owner.
8. **Population figures, decline rates or current legal status** for any religious community.
9. **A biography for Dadu Dayal** beyond his naming and the presence of his dohas in the Adi Granth.
10. **A separate Dvaita salvation formula** - the evidence gives a school and a lineage, and the lineage chain is written instead.
11. **Sikh doctrinal and institutional history** beyond the literary and devotional material named in Lesson 16.
12. **Constitutional provisions, comparative secularism theory and contemporary policy** on religious pluralism - handed to the polity and society papers, with the boundary stated in Lesson 20.

## Source-use statement

- **The Core owner** supplied the topic's spine: vocabulary, classification, the Bhakti causal model and rosters, the women's bank, the literature engine, the Sufi bank, the Vedanta bank, the philosophy-and-monuments bank, the smaller-communities bank, the festivals bank, the verdict scaffolds and the full factual-risk control list. Every one of its sections is mapped in Coverage Matrix section B.
- **The Advanced owner** supplied the analytical apparatus rather than new facts: the mechanism-and-limit thesis, vernacularisation as an expanded public, opposition as a defining feature, patronage-dependent growth, gendered participation without tokenism, the comparative framework, the festivals qualification and the verification caution on four schools. Every one of its sections is mapped in Coverage Matrix section C.
- **The single core OCR source** supplied the ancient-period cultural evidence that the Markdown owners assume rather than state: the late Vedic reaction and the compilation of the Upanishads; Mahavira's and the Buddha's chronology; the five Jaina doctrines and three gems; the Jaina language decision and its literary consequences; the Pataliputra council and the sectarian split; Jaina patronage in Kalinga and Karnataka; the eightfold path, the middle path and the sangha's openness and limits; Pali and its abandonment; the emergence of Mahayana with Kanishka's council; Gandhara and Mathura; the five recorded causes of Buddhist decline; the seventh-century spread of the devotional cult with its offering-and-prasada description; the sixth-century appearance of tantric practice; the post-Gupta rise of regional languages and scripts; Panini's Ashtadhyayi; and the philosophical-systems passage including Samkhya's means of knowledge and the Charvaka position.
- **Locally held official question papers** supplied every reproduced question, its response choices where present, its marks and its word limit. **No official answer key is held locally for the 2022 or 2023 Preliminary papers; the 2026 Preliminary key held locally is provisional. No answer letter is recorded anywhere in this file, and none has been inferred.** Where a paper's numbering is not legible at the point of an item, the item is identified without a serial under the policy stated at the head of the PYQ index.
- **Five bounded live checks, across two stages**, supplied the single current record and four factual refinements: in stage one, the Deepavali inscription and the exact register carrying Ramcharitmanas, verified against Ministry of Culture, Press Information Bureau and United Nations in India records; in stage two, the *Tevaram*'s composers and compiler, verified against standard reference-encyclopaedia entries and the Sahapedia article on Tamil Shaivite devotional hymns; the formation of the Ashtachhap, verified against Encyclopaedia Britannica, a standard reference-encyclopaedia entry and the Pushtimarg community body's own listing; and the *Ain-i-Akbari* silsilah count, for which **only secondary compilations were available** and which is therefore reported rather than asserted. No live source supplied any teaching claim beyond these five points.
- No artifact under the permanently excluded package directory was read, searched, cited or relied upon, and no learner-v2 artifact was used.
